### 3.21 Input channel = DTLS/UDP on `:base+12` — **RESOLVED, INPUT WORKING** `[C95+]`
- **Source**: capture `captures/captures_inputport_20260821_141446/` + visual validation through an H.264 dump of the remote screen.
- **Input is applied inside the VM**: movements, clicks, drags AND keyboard. Visual proofs:
  - a **drag-select** from (400,400) to (1400,750) -> a block of text highlighted exactly along that diagonal in the Firefox page, plus the tooltip of the link hovered at the end point;
  - **F11** -> Firefox switches to full screen (tab bar, address bar and taskbar disappear), then comes back on the next shot. Reproduced 3 times.
- **The full chain of fixes** (each one necessary):
  1. **Transport**: DTLS/UDP on `:base+12`, not TLS/TCP on `:base+14` (S11). `:base+14` carries only the ComChan and accepts only ONE TLS session.
  2. **`@83 = 0x01`** in the 96 B Connect (S14). With `0x02` the server stays SILENT: 0 datagrams after the handshake.
  3. **`@122`** = 1 on press / 0 on release (K3). At 0, every key looked like an isolated release.
  4. **`@96`** = a **per-device** counter (K5), not a global one: the keyboard restarts at 1 while the mouse's keeps going (mouse 189/190/191, keyboard 1..9, mouse 192/193 — the second number being `@40`, the message counter, which is global).
  5. **Left-button release** (C2): without it the button stays down and everything becomes a drag. A/B: `release=0` -> the blue selection persists and grows; `release=1` -> a clean click. Yet the desktop emits no message on a left release, so its `(106=16,140=0,150=0)` is most likely a complete click event, not a mere press.
- **The LEFT click's release is a MOVEMENT message** `[C95+]` (C8, 2026-08-21) — this is what was blocking everything. We were looking for it among the 152 B click messages, where it does not exist: the left button emits only ONE message per complete cycle there (verified by counting across 3 guided captures). It is in fact carried by a **144 B movement message with `@127 = 2`** instead of 1.
  - Proof 1, temporal split of the holds: 152 B on press, 144 B on release (a 12.7 s hold -> 152 B at 0.19 s then 144 B at 13.36 s; a 0.5 s hold -> 152 B at 0.44 s then 144 B at 0.52 s).
  - Proof 2, correlation: the **10** 144 B messages carrying `@127=2` each follow a real left release, 10 for 10, with an offset of +15 to +1131 ms (Shadow's latency).
  - Proof 3, a functional A/B: clicking the "Accept" button of a cookie banner — `SHADOW_INPUT_LEFT_RELEASE_MOVE=0` leaves the banner, `=1` accepts it and makes it disappear. Likewise, a click on a link really navigates.
  - User-side symptom before the fix: "the left click stays down and never releases", then "the picture ended up freezing" — the VM stayed in a permanent drag and its desktop stopped responding, while the stream was still running at ~29 fps. Right and middle worked, because their `(18,8,0)` release does exist.
- **The SCROLL WHEEL is a message family in its own right** `[C95+]` (W1, 2026-08-21) — guided capture `captures/captures_scroll_20260821_174331` (124 real notches, timestamped ground truth). Across 126 messages of 152 B, **125 fall within 1.5 s of a notch**, only one is an outlier.
  - Despite the size being identical to the clicks', they have nothing in common: the combination `(106=0, 140=0, 150=240)` where a click is `(16, ·, ·)`.
  - **The delta is a float32 LE at `@148`**: `+120.0` upwards, `-120.0` downwards. The sign matches the real direction **125 times out of 125**. `@144-147` = a constant `08 00 00 00`.
  - This family uses the **widened vtable** (`@16 = 0x20`), so the following fields are shifted by 4: the event counter is at `@100`, not `@96`. Hence a dedicated emitter — the click patcher would write at the wrong offsets.
  - A stable template: across the 125 messages, only `@100` varies apart from the timestamp and the delta. No coordinates — the server already knows the cursor's position. Our frames are now identical to a real one to the bit.
- **The 144 B message is not a "movement"** but a generic event carrier `[C95+]`. In the browsing session of 2026-08-21: movements (1225, `@95=1`/`@127=1`), keystrokes (70, `@95=2`, `@110` = 6/0), left releases (13, `@127=2`). Reasoning in terms of "click messages" versus "movement messages" is what made us look for the left release in the wrong place.
- **Window resizing: NO resolution renegotiation** `[C95+]` (R1, 2026-08-21) — guided capture `captures/captures_resize_20260821_175354`, 35 resizes with geometry sampled every 200 ms.
  - The official client emits **no control message** on a resize: no `kNotifyResolution` (f14), no `kDisplayConfig` (f10) beyond the bootstrap. The stream's resolution does not move; **the client scales locally** (which matches the user's observation: the picture keeps its aspect ratio).
  - What it does send is a **one-byte `0x64` message on `:base+20`** (VideoSslTcpChannel): **92 during resizes, 0 outside them**. It is a **frame refresh request**, emitted when the local scaling changes. `0x64` was already known (sent 3× at connect time as a "trigger") but its meaning was not.
  - `0x70` on the same channel remains the heartbeat (13 occurrences, spread out).
  - Corollary: `SHADOW_SEND_NRES` (f14) can stay at 0 — resizing was not its trigger. Our GUI already letterboxes locally; all it lacks is the call to `ctrl_video_tcp_request_refresh()` when the render area changes.
- **Measurement lesson**: aiming at a small button (Firefox's "+") is a bad test — it fails for reasons of focus or precision and makes you wrongly conclude that nothing gets through. **Drag-select** and **F11** are unambiguous. Likewise, a taskbar icon being highlighted is Windows 11's *active application* indicator, not a hover.
- **Tooling**: `SHADOW_DUMP_H264=1` + `ffmpeg -update 1` gives a picture of the remote screen; a harness `SHADOW_INPUT_CLICK_AT` / `SHADOW_INPUT_CLICK_XY` / `SHADOW_INPUT_DRAG` running in a **closed loop** on the position the server sends back (`ctrl_input_tcp_server_pos`) — indispensable because the VM keeps the cursor's position from one session to the next.
- **Coordinate space**: it follows the session's negotiated resolution. Our session (DisplayConfig 1920x1080) accepts (1900,1000) and refuses (2400,1400); the X values up to 2494 in the capture came from its own resolution.
- **The wire is verified byte-exact** for all three types: a cross-diff over 65 real keystrokes (`@142` the only byte for the key, `@110`/`@122` the only ones for the state), 22 mouse/keyboard discriminating bytes identical on both sides, clicks validated against the evdev ground truth of two guided captures.
- **The server does not discriminate against us**: its Authentication reply contains a per-stream table (`f5` repeated, f1 = 0..7, f2 = a binary value) **identical** to the desktop's — streams 0-3 `00 01`, 4-5 `00`, 6-7 `01`.
- Refs: `core/protocol/ctrl_input_tcp.c`, `core/protocol/ctrl_session.c`, `core/protocol/ctrl_audio_dtls.c`
- Cross-ref: §3.19, §3.20, §3.22, §3.23

### 3.22 Byte-exact bootstrap — Authentication and kHid fixed `[C95+]`
- **Source**: a dump of our own bytes (`SHADOW_DUMP_AUTH`) diffed field by field against the desktop capture.
- **Authentication (f4)** was 309 B against 245 B. Two causes, both fixed (S4):
  - `permissions` = 250 instead of **510 (0x1fe)**: `AUDIO_IN (0x004)` and `FILE_TRANSFER (0x100)` were missing. The desktop sets all 8 bits.
  - `channels_per_stream` (f7): F3 declared **9** stream types (including `Input -> UDP`, when the input goes over TCP); the official client declares **only one**, `Video -> UDP`. The difference is exactly +64 B. F3 was a hypothesis ("don't look entry-tier") never checked against the capture.
  - Result: 245 B against 245 B, structure and lengths identical field by field.
- **kHid (f6, case 6 of the oneof)**: the desktop emits `f6 { f1{f1=1}, f2{}, f3{} }` (a 99 B frame); we were omitting `f3{}` (a 97 B frame). Fixed (S5, `SHADOW_HID_F3`).
- **Neither one unblocks the input**: an A/B on the I9 harness (Windows key -> a video bitrate spike) gives 1.18× against 1.15×, i.e. the VBR ramp in both cases. These are conformance gains.
- Refs: `core/protocol/ctrl_msgs.c`, `core/protocol/ctrl_msgs.h`, `core/protocol/ctrl_session.c`
- Cross-ref: §3.21


### 3.23 Encryption is ASYMMETRIC in keys — the uplink key is OURS `[C95+]`
- **Source**: capture `captures/captures_inputport_20260821_141446/`, offline decryption (`cryptography`), with a positive control on video and cursor.
- Shadow uses **two distinct chacha20-poly1305 keys**, one per direction:
  - **server -> client**: the 32 B key from the `Encryption` **reply** (f12: `f1`=32 keylen, `f2`=12 noncelen, `f3`=16 taglen, `f4`=key). Positive control: a `:base+10` video packet decrypts to 1241 B beginning with `02 a4 aa ad` (= the documented `VideoFrame` header), and a `:base+30` cursor packet to 271 B. **[Label SUPERSEDED on 2026-09-11: that 271 B of cleartext is an Opus session's stream descriptor (type `0x02`), not a cursor packet — `:base+30` is AudioOut alone (§3.37), see §3.26. The positive control on decryption stands.]**
  - **client -> server**: the 32 B key **the client itself sends** in its `Encryption` **request**, same f12 structure. Our code generated it then **threw it away** — the comment described it as an unimportant "client_random / DH share".
- Proof: the 42 B packet emitted on `:base+13` **does not decrypt** with the reply's key (neither as AEAD nor as a raw stream) but decrypts **perfectly** with the request's.
- Converging clue: video and cursor share the same nonce suffix (`e04dc3a3b0230db0558a1c`, byte 0 = a counter) while `:base+13` has a different one (`b7d54df1d4d1f8789b65e0`) — two nonce spaces, hence two distinct streams.
- **Content of the `:base+13` keepalive**: a **constant 14 B payload** `04 00 05 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00`, emitted every **~7 s**, with only the nonce's counter varying. Wire = `[ct 14][nonce 12][tag 16]`.
- **Fixed (K11/K12)**: `ctrl_build_encryption_request` now returns the generated key, `shadow_cipher_create(client_key, server_key)` (we were passing the server key on both sides), and the `:base+13` keepalive is genuinely encrypted. The old code sent 42 B **of random data** with a frozen header copied from an old capture — its own comment said "will fail decrypt". Key generation moves from an unseeded `rand()` to wolfSSL's RNG.
- `encryption.h` already provided for two Tx/Rx keys ("the Linux binary's CryptoCipher layout"): the API was right, only the caller was wrong.
- **Did not unblock input injection** (cf. §3.21) — it is a conformance gain.
- Refs: `streaming/ctrl_msgs.c::ctrl_build_encryption_request`, `core/protocol/ctrl_session.c`, `core/protocol/encryption.h`
- Cross-ref: §3.21, §3.15


### 3.24 Client menu settings -> wire `[C95+]`
- **Source**: guided capture `captures/captures_settings_20260821_180409` (`tools/capture_scroll_resize_run.sh settings`), analysed by `tools/analyze_settings.py`. Every setting change is timestamped.
- An earlier RE session (TIER9-W4) had established that presets are mapped **client-side** onto the existing fields, never naming the preset on the wire. What was missing: WHICH fields, and by what mechanism.
- **Three distinct mechanisms depending on the setting**:

| setting | message | effect |
|---|---|---|
| **bitrate** | `kUpdateSession` (**f13**) | applied **live**, with no interruption |
| **codec H.264 -> HEVC** | `kUnregisterSession` (f9) + a new **channel announcement** (f8) | renegotiation of the video channel alone |
| **codec back / Color 4:4:4** | `kRestart` (**f5**, an **EMPTY** message) | a **full restart**: re-Authentication + Encryption + DisplayConfig + the 8 announcements |

- **Structure of the bitrate message**: `f13 { f1 { f2 = 794816, f4 = bitrate_bps } }`. `f2` is CONSTANT (identical across both observed changes); the bitrate is in **f4**. Measured: "low" bitrate -> `f4 = 1000000`, "unlimited" -> `f4 = 70000000`.
- **`kRestart` (f5) is an empty message**: `f2 { f5 sub(0 B) }`. It carries no parameter — it is the new bootstrap that carries the new configuration.
- **Our message was malformed** `[fixed by S18]`: we wrote the bitrate into **f2** and added an `f3` float fps that does not exist in the real message. So the server read our bitrate as the constant field and never saw a value in f4 — **our bitrate requests could never have taken effect**. This **invalidates the "server ceiling ~14 Mbps" measurement** in `memory/project_quality_params_VALIDATED.md`, which rested on that message.
- **Also corrects a K15 conclusion**: f13 had been switched off after observing that the desktop never emits it — true in captures where nobody touched the settings. It emits it on **every bitrate change**. Default back to ON with the correct structure.
- **The account's real configuration**: the official session negotiates **2560x1440 @ 60 fps @ 55 Mbps** (70 Mbps on "unlimited"), where our `CI_BODY_5` is frozen at 1920x1080 @ 143.9 fps @ 20 Mbps — values copied from a capture made on another machine with other settings. A picture-quality lead in its own right.
- **Not exposed in the v13.1.5 menu**: the frame rate limit, present in our notes from old screenshots, does not exist.
- **A/B measurements of our video parameters** (2026-08-21, a near-static desktop):

| config | announced fps | requested bitrate | achieved bitrate |
|---|---|---|---|
| current defaults | 143.9 | 20 / 15 Mbps | 2664 – 2806 kbps (3 runs) |
| 60 fps + 55 Mbps | 60 | 55 Mbps | 1452 – 1466 kbps (3 runs) |
| 143.9 fps + 55 Mbps | 143.9 | 55 Mbps | 2323 – 2433 kbps (2 runs) |
| 60 fps + default | 60 | 20 / 15 Mbps | 2457 – **3558** kbps (2 runs) |

  - **Asking for more bitrate does NOT increase the throughput** on this kind of content, and the last row shows 45 % variance between two identical runs. The encoder produces what the content demands. Conclusion: **bitrate on a static desktop is a bad indicator** — a reminder of the same trap as `SHADOW_PARITY_TRIM` (cf. the Build/Tests section). A valid test must be **visual and on moving content**; we can now produce a reproducible one with our own scroll wheel, which works.
  - **The RESOLUTION, on the other hand, is accepted and effective** `[C95+]`: with `--width=2560 --height=1440`, the received stream really is 2560x1440 (verified with `ffprobe`) and shows the desktop at its native size, with no resampling — 8074 kbps, 799 frames for 799 displayed, 100 % decryption. It is our `DisplayConfig` that sets the VM's render resolution; our 1920x1080 was only a leftover from an old capture. The setting does not persist between sessions.
  - Practical consequence: on Switch, keep 1920x1080 or less (decoding 1440p would be more expensive for a 720p/1080p display); on desktop, targeting the native resolution makes sense.
- Refs: `streaming/ctrl_msgs.c::ctrl_build_video_encoding_config_ex`, `tools/analyze_settings.py`
- Cross-ref: §3.21

### 3.25 GAMEPAD — native path on `:base+13` `[C95+]`
- **Source**: guided capture `captures/captures_gamepad_20260821_183535` (a Bluetooth DualShock 4, 777 timestamped evdev events, a route isolating each input). Decryption: **238/238**.
- **The gamepad goes through `:base+13` over UDP chacha20-poly1305**, format `[ct 14][nonce 12][tag 16]`, with the uplink key (the one from OUR `Encryption` request, cf. §3.23). It is the same channel as the keepalive — which was therefore only one of its messages.
- **It does NOT go through the input channel `:base+12`**: throughout the gamepad session, all 218 of the 144 B messages there carry the mouse-movement signature. **No additional connection** is opened: the channel set is the standard one (9010-9015, 9020, 9030, 9032).
- **No `shadowusb` daemon**: the DualShock 4 is recognised as soon as the stream opens and forwarded natively. This **reopens the Joy-Cons route on Switch**, where the Spice/usbredir lead had been abandoned (§2.5).
- **Payload format (14 bytes)**: `04 [b1] [type] ...` — `b1 = 0` except on the very first message of the session, `04 01 03 02 …`, which is the **plug announcement** (the native equivalent of the web path's `GamepadPluggedInputV4Model`).

| type (`@2`) | meaning | encoding |
|---|---|---|
| `00` | **button** | `@11` = identifier, `@13` = 1 pressed / 0 released — **full table below** |
| `01` | **axis** | `@11` = axis index (00 to 05 observed), **`@12` = value on 0-255** |
| `02` | **d-pad** | value at `@3` (`01` = up held, `00` = released) |
| `05` | **keepalive** | zeroed body, ~7 s (§3.23) |

- **COMPLETE button table** `[C95+]` — capture `captures/captures_gamepad-libre_20260821_184828`, 2598/2598 packets decrypted, 100 % consistent correspondence (each button has exactly one identifier, `@13` follows the state without exception):

| `@11` | button | `@11` | button |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | Square | 6 | R1 |
| 1 | Triangle | 7 | L1 |
| 2 | Cross | 8 | Start |
| 3 | Circle | 9 | Select |
| 4 | R3 | 10 | Guide (PS) |
| 5 | L3 | | |

  L2 and R2 do not appear there: they are **analogue triggers**, hence transmitted as axes.
- **`@12` carries the axis value on the 0-255 scale**, the same one as evdev — established by two clean correspondences (mean deviation ~10 against 65-113 for noise): **wire axis 0 -> evdev code 3**, **wire axis 4 -> evdev code 2**.
- **COMPLETE axis mapping** `[C95+]` — capture `captures/captures_gamepad-axes_20260821_192400` (`tools/capture_scroll_resize_run.sh gamepad-axes`: a visualisation with a target to reach, automatic calibration of the evdev codes, 14/14 targets held). Correlated over the full time series:

| wire index (`@11`) | axis | mean deviation |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | RX | 0.1 |
| 1 | LY | 24.7 |
| 2 | LX | 0.1 |
| 3 | RY | 25.6 |
| 4 | L2 | 1.4 |
| 5 | R2 | 1.5 |

  The vertical axes are noisier but resolve by elimination (LX and RX being assigned with a deviation of 0.1). All in the **direct** sense, no inversion.
- **Role of `@13`**: it is not a checksum. The difference `@13 - @12` is **constant per axis and equals 128** (127 or 129 depending on the axis): `@12` and `@13` carry **the same value in two representations**, one centred on zero, the other on 128. Measured: wire 0 and 2 -> +128 systematically; wire 1 and 3 -> +127 (89 and 102 times out of 91 and 104); wire 4 and 5 -> +129/+130.
- **Design trap worth remembering**: holding a stick still produces **no** message — the client only emits on change. So a capture of "held positions" gives almost nothing; it is the **movements** that carry the information, and you have to correlate over the full time series.
- **Still to be pinned down (old)**: the mapping of the other 4 axis indices, and the exact role of `@13` (it follows `@12` with a per-axis offset — `+0x80` for axis 2, `+0x7f` for 3, `+0x81` for 4 — hence most likely a checksum, not a mirror). Both require a capture in which **only one axis moves at a time**: stick diagonals make the pairing ambiguous.
- **Tooling trap**: the gamepad exposes a second evdev device, "… Motion Sensors", which uses **the same axis codes 0-5** with values outside the 0..255 range. Without filtering it, the gyroscope drowns out any correlation. Fixed in `capture_scroll_resize.py`. Likewise, do not trust an assumed table of ABS labels: on this DualShock 4 it turned out to be wrong — **use the raw code**.
- **Still to be pinned down (old)**: the exact encoding of the axis value. `@13` is often `@12 + 0x80` but not always (`1e`->`9f`, `fe`->`7f`), so it is not a simple mirror — to be resolved by a fine correlation against the evdev values, which the capture already contains.
- **Implication for the port**: the implementation is within reach — one more UDP socket, the same encryption already in place, and 14-byte payloads. No FlatBuffers, no new TLS channel.
- Refs: `tools/capture_scroll_resize_run.sh gamepad`, `tools/analyze_gamepad.py`
- Cross-ref: §3.21, §3.23, §2.5

### 3.26 AUDIO — multiplexed on the cursor channel `:base+30` — `[C95+]` RESOLVED
> **Title SUPERSEDED (§3.37, 2026-08-26; reminder of 2026-09-11)**: `:base+30` is **AudioOut alone**; "cursor channel" is only its old name in our code (`on_cursor_packet`, `udp_cursor_pkts`). The `0x02` cleartexts we took for cursors there are the stream descriptor (below).
- **Source**: guided capture `captures/captures_audio_20260821_223137` (silence/sound, `tools/capture_audio_run.sh`) + client validation (sound audible and stable, ×0.99 real time, 0 errors)

**Where** — `:base+30`, the cursor channel. Decisive contrast: 19.8 → 170.3 packets/s as soon as the remote desktop plays sound. No extra socket opens. `:base+12` is the INPUT (refuted by measurement: 1146 packets, all 104 B, with a video playing) and `:base+15` is SSH/SFTP.

**Frame** — an 11-byte SUFP header identical to the video's (`[0x23][counter][0][0][u32 id][0x01]`), then `[ct][nonce 12][tag 16]` chacha20-poly1305, the session key, nonce `[u16 LE counter][10 B static]`.

**Cleartext** — `[0x12][u32 LE sequence]` then the RAW Opus packet at byte 5. Observed TOC `0xf4` = CELT fullband, **stereo**, one 10 ms frame → 480 samples at 48 kHz, which is what the channel announces (48000/16). The 8-byte frames are NOT cursor positions: they are very short Opus frames (silence).

**Stream descriptor (2026-09-11, DEC-1)** — `[C95+]` Every `:base+30` stream opens with a cleartext of type `0x02`, sent TWICE with sequence number 0: **13 bytes in a FLAC session** (`02 00 00 00 00 01 00 02 00 10 80 bb 02`), **271 bytes in an Opus session** (`02 00 00 00 00 01 00 01 00 10 80 bb 02 01 01 …`). Proofs, FLAC baseline of 2026-09-11: `[AUD2] trame audio #1 len=13` in session 1, `[AUD8] doublon seq=0 ... len 13` in session 8. Bytes 9-12: 16 bits, 48000 Hz (`80 bb`, u16 LE), 2 channels. **Byte 7 is 1 in every Opus session and 2 in every FLAC session**: the observation is `[C95+]`, its reading as the CODEC is `[C80]` — it is the enum of the announcement (K14) and of the grant (§3.49), 1 Opus, 2 FLAC, but only two codecs have been seen. The Opus version's 258 extra bytes are not decoded. **This is not audio** — only `0x12` is (`core/protocol/audio_route.h`): passed to the decoder, it cost one false error per Opus session and froze FLAC sessions into Opus (§9, DEC-1). The client logs it once per session, `[AUD2] descripteur de flux : 16 bits 48000 Hz 2 voies octet7=1`, then discards it. It is also the "271 B cursor bitmap" of CUR1/RE2 (May 2026, `memory/`) and the "271 B `:base+30` cursor packet" of §3.23.

**Numbering (2026-09-11, AUD-DEDUP-3)** — `[C95+]` The `u32 LE` at bytes 1-4 counts the frames the server SENDS: +1 per frame emitted, both copies sharing the number. It is not a time index: on a muted VM, `[AUD8]` shows numbers 1..7 strictly consecutive ~400 ms apart (8 sessions); with continuous sound, the `[AUD17]` summary counts ~6480 deltas of 1 over ~6480 frames accepted per session. So a gap in the numbering is a loss, and silence leaves none. The server renumbers on every stream (§3.31).

**Split frames (FLAC with sound)** — `[C95+]` A FLAC frame too large for one datagram goes out in pieces, reassembled by byte 1 (AUD12); the two copies of a split frame arrive one AFTER the other on the wire (`decoupees: ok=952` = half of `multi=1904`, 20 s of music). A muted VM produces none, and Opus never does.

**Three conditions, all necessary**:
1. **Ping the channel**: a `0x70` byte every 7.50 s, after the 25 B registration. The same cadence as the input keepalive (K2).
2. **Release the previous session**: without a `kUnregisterSession` (oneof f9) on close, the server keeps the subscription and the channel stays SILENT on reconnection — the video, by contrast, comes back. That is what gave one session in two with no sound and no cursor.
3. **De-duplicate**: every frame is emitted TWICE (measured: 200 frames/s for 10 ms frames, and `dup` = exactly half of `audio` when working). A sliding window of 64 sequence numbers; a simple comparison against the last number would let one frame in two through if the duplicates alternate.

**Decoding trap** — `opus_decode` accepts badly framed packets and returns NOISE with no error: that is what led to the wrong conclusion that offset 0 was correct. Validate with `opus_packet_get_nb_samples` BEFORE decoding.

**Output-side reading** — decoding runs on the thread that receives UDP and the frames arrive in bursts: writing straight into ALSA overflows its buffer (795 losses out of 1616), then empties it. A half-second ring + a dedicated playback thread; only that thread is allowed to block.

- **Why the question stayed open for months**: no earlier capture contained sound — the remote desktop stayed silent throughout every RE session. A permanently active channel (the cursor) perfectly masks a channel that only lights up with sound.
- Refs: `core/protocol/ctrl_session.c` (AUD2/4/5/6/8), `core/protocol/ctrl_msgs.c` (`ctrl_build_unregister_session`), `core/media/audio.c` (AUD7/9)
- Cross-ref: §3.21, §3.19 (**SUPERSEDED**: its "DTLS audio" is the input)

### 3.27 `gE` — congestion control feedback on `:base+10` — `[C90]`
- **Source**: `captures/captures_audio_20260821_223137`, 1799 specimens analysed
- The official client emits **20 times per second** (every ~50 ms) a 15-byte message on the VIDEO port:

  `[0x67 0x45 0x01][field1 u32 LE][field2 i32 LE][field3 u32 LE]`

- **field1 `[C90]`**: an estimate of the receive bitrate in **BYTES PER SECOND**, smoothed. Determined in steady state from the official capture: 146,564 announced for 10,610 bytes received in 70 ms, i.e. 151,571 B/s — a 3 % difference. During ramps the value lags (smoothing), which explains the aberrant ratios an instantaneous measurement gives. **We were sending `bytes × 4`, i.e. five times less than reality over a 50 ms interval** (G14): a control loop fed such an underestimate keeps the encoder right at the floor.
- **field2**: small SIGNED values (+280, -1, +13, +23, -45, +87) — a delay-difference / jitter measurement. **We always send 0.**
- **field3**: a timestamp in microseconds (deltas of ~45,000 for 50 ms intervals).
- The official client also emits on this port: `70` (1 byte) and `50 49 01` every second, and `72 47 01 <nonce> <count> 00 [u16 LE × N]` (`rG`, retransmission) — **rarely**, but with up to 92 indices at once, where our implementation caps at 32.
- **Our `rG` cannot repair anything**: it carries no frame id (the format has none), and it is limited to one send every 100 ms — i.e. six frames later at 65 fps. So it names chunk indices of a frame the server has already forgotten.
- Refs: `core/protocol/ctrl_session.c` (gE block, ~L2438), `tools/ida/out/` (remote bitrate estimator)
- Cross-ref: §3.16 (G4), §3.15

### 3.28 The server only sends a key frame on the FIRST session — you have to ask for it — `[C95+]` RESOLVED
- **Source**: an automated series of 20 consecutive sessions on console, 2026-08-25 (`autotest 20 25 5`, full network log). Campaign S34.
- **Central fact**: on the very first session the server spontaneously emits SPS (NAL 7) + PPS (8) + IDR (5). **On reconnection it does not do it again**: it continues the GOP in progress and sends nothing but type 1 NALs. Measured: trials 3 to 20 = **type 1 exclusively**, across 30 NALs logged per trial.
- Consequence: the decoder discards everything until its first key frame (`h264_decoder.c::ready_to_decode`, "gate opened on IDR"). With no request from us, the gate never opens → **a black screen with a perfectly normal inbound bitrate** (18,259 packets, 22 MB, `decoded=1009` reassembled units, `perdus=0`).
- **The request works**: trial 1's IFRs `69 50 00 02 [u16 LE ctr]` (t=11.7 to 16.9 s) did produce SPS+IDR ~0.5 s later. The mechanism was never at fault — its triggering was.
- **Two locks, both function `static`s that outlived the session**:
  1. `ctrl_session.c` — `g_last_evt_idr_tick` was compared against `ip_counter`, a LOCAL of `ctrl_session_run` that restarts at zero. After a 24 s session (~500 ticks), the next session computed `0 - 500 = -500`, never `>= 10`: **no request was emitted at all, for the rest of the process**. The same trap at the `uint16_t` wraparound (~55 min).
  2. `ctrl_session_glue.c` — the G13 stall detector tested `++calees == 15`, a strict equality, on a `calees` that was also static: it fired only **once per process**.
- The log counters (`g_ifr_log`, `ka13_log`, `nals_total`, G44's `logn`) were static too: mute from the 2nd session on. **That is what made the failure invisible across several campaigns** — the logs gave the impression that decoding was running.
- **Fix**: `video_feedback_t` (ctrl_session.c) and `idr_stall_t` (glue) group that state and reset it every session; the rate limit uses **modular** 16-bit arithmetic + a "never requested" flag; G13 **repeats** its request every 15 feeds; a key-frame request is emitted **right at the start of the session** (trial 2 had taken 15 s to display).
- **Secondary defect fixed along the way**: `g_gE_last_bytes_rx` (the `gE` bitrate feedback, §3.27) was static too and compared itself against `udp_video_bytes`, which restarts at zero. On the first tick of each new session the UNSIGNED subtraction wrapped and announced an absurd bitrate to the server (~4 GB/s). Grouped into `video_feedback_t`, hence fixed.
- The rules are isolated in the pure module `core/protocol/idr_policy.h`, covered by `tests/test_idr_policy.c` (17 checks, each with its counter-case). Verified: the old logic fails 6 of those checks.
- **VALIDATED ON CONSOLE** (a series of 10 sessions, 2026-08-25, after the fix): **10 trials out of 10 with a picture**, 1073 frames on average per 24 s session (~47 fps), against 2 out of 20 before. The decoder's gate now opens ~1 s after the session starts (against 15 s in trial 2, and never afterwards).
- Refs: `core/protocol/idr_policy.h`, `streaming/ctrl_session.c::session_video_feedback_tick`, `streaming/ctrl_session_glue.c::glue_decode_frame`, `tests/test_idr_policy.c`
- Cross-ref: §3.16 (G4), §7 (Switch)

### 3.29 ~~Cursor/audio channel `:base+30`~~ — **SUPERSEDED twice** — `[obsolete]`
> **Do not act on this section.** It is kept for the history of the refutations.
> 1. Its original title ("it only lights up when the VM produces sound") is **FALSE**: the user played a video on the VM and the channel stayed silent. I had drawn causation from a correlation.
> 2. The channel is not "cursor/audio": `:base+30` is **AudioOut alone** (§3.37). The "cursor packets" counted here were an artefact of `udp_cursor_pkts`, which counts EVERY packet on the port.
> 3. The real cause of the intermittency is **§3.38**: we were killing the channel ourselves.
> What remains useful below: the table of the seven eliminated hypotheses — it says where not to look.
- **Source**: automated series of 20 then 26 sessions, 2026-08-25.
- The channel appears active in ~38 % of sessions. **This is not an intermittent failure**: when it is active, the breakdown shows **3998 audio packets out of 4003** — only 5 cursor packets. So the channel carries almost nothing but audio, and it only lights up when the VM emits sound. A "silent" session is a session where the VM was playing nothing (the autotest runs on a silent desktop and does not move the mouse).
- Methodological corollary: **you cannot measure audio with the autotest as it stands**. You must guarantee a sound source on the VM, otherwise the absence of sound proves nothing. That is what had already dragged this subject out for months — cf. `project_audio_FOUND_cursor_channel.md`: "no capture had any sound".
- The earlier estimates ("never works on console", "1 in 10", "13/20") were therefore measuring the VM's activity, not the channel's health.
- The UDP registration is resent every 2 s as long as nothing arrives (AUD5): up to 20 resends observed with no effect, which **refutes** the lost-registration hypothesis.
- **SEVEN hypotheses eliminated by measurement on 2026-08-25** (campaigns S42-S44). The table below is the main result: it says where NOT to look.

  | hypothesis | test | result |
  |---|---|---|
  | "nothing to send" (silent VM) | mouse moved: 14 movements, **40 server echoes** on the input channel | REFUTED — the server tracks, but does not open `:base+30` |
  | incomplete "stream -> channel" table | `SHADOW_AUTH_FULL_MAP=1`: authentication 245 -> 309 B, **accepted** | REFUTED — the channel is still silent |
  | registration lost / too early | AUD5, 20 resends over 40 s | REFUTED |
  | bootstrap deviating | `SHADOW_BOOTSTRAP_EXACT=1`: the sequence made **identical** to the desktop capture (87 245 127 115 99 99 103 95 97 103 99 89) | REFUTED |
  | announcement content | byte-by-byte diff against the capture: **7 of 8 identical, including AUDIO**; only the video differs, on its dynamic values | REFUTED |
  | source port blacklisted server-side | S43: socket recreated on a fresh source port, 4 times | REFUTED |
  | byte-exact bootstrap + timing (S42+S47) | a series of 8: bootstrap made identical to the official client, messages AND timing (the State/Notify pair at 0.8 s instead of 3.4 s) | **REFUTED with power** — 3/8 (38 %) against a 3/10 (30 %) baseline, not a significant difference |
  | a connected socket filtering the source | S44: socket opened WITHOUT `connect()` | **REFUTED AND HARMFUL** — without `connect()` the socket no longer has a proper local address (`ss`: `UNCONN *:*` where video/input are `ESTAB` over IPv6) and the channel drops to ZERO. Default back to 0. |

- **DENOUEMENT (2026-08-25)**: the channel works. Two facts unblocked the situation, both supplied by the user: the remote desktop's wallpaper **emits sound continuously** (so every session should have some), and "we used to manage it" — so it was a REGRESSION, not a missing feature.
  - **The defect was reproducible on Linux**, same VM, same native code: `cursor=0` while the video received 86,745 packets. It was never console-specific — which cost hours of looking for it on console, one human manipulation per trial.
  - Cause: our own S44 of the same day. With `connect()` restored, the same test returns `cursor=5989 audio=5983 dup=2991` — **exactly half discarded**, which incidentally validates the S36 de-duplication fix (§3.31) — and the sound plays at **1.00× real time**.
  - **Methodological lesson**: check that a defect reproduces on desktop BEFORE treating it as console-specific. The same code runs there, and the loop costs a command instead of a manipulation.
  - The intermittency PRIOR to S44 (channel active 3/10 then 7/16) remains unexplained — to be measured now that the baseline is sound.

- **What remains**: our behaviour on this channel is now indistinguishable from the official client's — the same announcements, the same bootstrap, the same traffic, a permissive socket. The server answers everywhere else. The untested variable is no longer in the client but in the **VM / the account**: the reference capture comes from ANOTHER VM (base 8000) on another date. The next measurement to make: check that an OFFICIAL client gets sound from THAT VM, now. If not, the subject is not our client.
- Cross-ref: §3.26

### 3.30 The UDP receive buffer is capped at 42,240 bytes on console — `[C95+]`
- **Source**: the validation series' log, 2026-08-25 — `udp_register: :10010 SO_RCVBUF set=4194304 actual=42240` in EVERY session.
- `setsockopt(SO_RCVBUF, 4 MB)` (fix N56) **has no effect on Switch**: the `bsdsockets` driver refuses to go beyond what its init configuration reserved. The value obtained, 42,240, is exactly `0xA500` — libnx's default `udp_rx_buf_size` (documented as "typically 0xA500" in `switch/runtime/devices/socket.h`).
- Borealis sets that configuration in `library/borealis/library/lib/platforms/switch/switch_wrapper.c::userAppInit`: it starts from `socketGetDefaultInitConfig()` and only raises `num_bsd_sessions` (12) and `sb_efficiency` (8) — **not** the buffer sizes.
- Measured consequence: a real UDP loss of ~0.7 % (`perdus=78/11210` over 20 s), which in turn triggers a key frame every ~500 ms through G26. N56 had established that 208 KB was already enough to saturate on desktop; 42 KB is five times less.
- **This is not G5's autofocus**: where G5/G37 described IDRs on PHANTOM errors at `perdus=0`, here the loss is real and the IDR request is the correct response. Treat the cause (the buffer), not the symptom (the IDRs).
- **FIX AND VALIDATION** (2026-08-25): `core/services/sockets_compat.c`, the `__SWITCH__` branch — we close the socket driver opened by `userAppInit` and reopen it with `udp_rx_buf_size = 1 MB`, at the very start of `main`, before any network I/O. Borealis is not modified (the library is vendored as is). Falls back to the original configuration if HOS refuses; `SHADOW_UDP_RXBUF=0` disables it.
- A/B measurement on console, two complete series of 24 s sessions:

  | | before (42 KB) | after (1 MB) |
  |---|---|---|
  | UDP loss | **0.460 %** (9 sessions) | **0.111 %** (8 sessions) |
  | packets received / session | 13,625 | 18,864 (+38 %) |
  | frames / session | 1073 | 1182 (+10 %) |
  | trials with a picture | 10/10 | 8/8 |

  Loss **divided by 4.1**. The log confirms `SO_RCVBUF set=4194304 actual=1048576` in every session, against `actual=42240` before.
- Cross-ref: §3.28, `project_N56_udp_rcvbuf_FIX.md`


### 3.31 Sound was only audible on the FIRST session — the de-duplication window was kept — `[C95+]` RESOLVED
- **Source**: automated series, 2026-08-25 (campaign S36). User symptom: "I only heard the audio once".
- The server emits every Opus frame **twice** on `:base+30`. We keep a sliding window of 64 sequence numbers so only one is played (AUD8).
- **That state lived in a `static` inside the receive function** (`seq_hi`, `seq_seen`, `seq_init`), so it outlived the session. And the server **renumbers on every stream**: the next session sent much lower numbers, `(int32_t)(seq - seq_hi)` came to ~-1,000,000, hence "far too late" — and **every** frame was discarded.
- **Decisive measurement**, broken down per session (`audio_dup_skipped` vs `udp_audio_pkts`):

  | session | audio frames | discarded as duplicates |
  |---|---|---|
  | first with sound | 3998 | **1999** (one in two — correct) |
  | all the following ones | 3997 | **3997 (100 %)** |

- Fix: `audio_dedup_t` in the session context (reset per session), with the rule isolated in the pure module `core/protocol/audio_dedup.h`, covered by `tests/test_audio_dedup.c` (14 checks, including one that **demonstrates** that reusing the previous window plays no frame at all).
- The third occurrence of the same family after §3.28 and §3.30: **session state kept in a function `static`**. In the session path, a `static` is suspect by default.
- **2026-09-11 — the window resynchronises, and its counters change meaning** `[C95+]` (§9: ING-A1, ING-A4, DEC-1, AUD-DEDUP-3, AUD-LC-3, AUD-INS-1, AUD-INS-3):
  - eight consecutive packets beyond the window re-prime it (ING-A1; `SHADOW_AUDIO_DEDUP_RESYNC=0` restores this paragraph's rule): an inherited window, or one pushed too far, no longer refuses everything, it realigns within 8 packets, for ~30 ms of sound. `tests/test_audio_dedup.c` goes from 14 to 34 checks, and this paragraph's demonstration runs there under `no_resync`;
  - `udp_audio_pkts` (the stats line's `audio=`) counts EVERY frame that reaches the window, duplicates included, on every path — single packet, reassembly by byte 1, and the SUFP fallback when `SHADOW_AUD_SUFP=1` turns it back on; `audio= - dup=` = frames played (ING-A4). The table above comes from the single-packet path, which already counted that way: it stays comparable;
  - `audio=` (stats and census) loses the stream descriptor's two copies per session, `dup=` one (DEC-1); the first `[AUD8]` check reports `seq=1`;
  - `dup=` still counts ALL refusals; those 64 numbers or more behind the window — this paragraph's class — are additionally counted separately, as `aud_vieux=` at the end of the stats line, next to `aud_perdues=`, the frames lost in both copies, definitive 64 frames later (AUD-DEDUP-3);
  - the `[AUD2] :+30` census, the `trame audio #` dumps, the `#` numbers of `[AUD11]` and `[AUD15]` and the 15 s sentinel all restart from zero every session (AUD-LC-3);
  - new lines: `[AUD2] descripteur de flux` (one per session), `[ING-A1]`, `[ING-A2]`, `[DEC1]`, `[AUD17]` (`trou audio`, `pendant la coupure video`, `bilan de session`), `audio: bilan de session` (one per decoder); the 5 s stats line gains `aud_perdues=` and `aud_vieux=` after `ecart_max=`, and the glue's line gains ` aud_pub=` after `jetees=` (AUD-INS-3);
  - panel: "Frames/s" is drawn in a neutral colour — it counts frames SENT, 2.5 to 5 per second on a muted VM — and health reads on "Frames lost", shown only when non-zero (AUD-INS-1).
- Refs: `core/protocol/audio_dedup.h`, `streaming/ctrl_session.c::on_cursor_packet`, `tests/test_audio_dedup.c`
- Cross-ref: §3.26, §3.28, §3.29


### 3.32 Crash on the first key-frame resend on `:base+20` — `&ctx` on a pointer — `[C95+]` RESOLVED
- **Source**: Atmosphère crash report `01787687738`, resolved against the EXACT binary that was running, 2026-08-25 (campaign S37).
- **A regression we introduced** while extracting `session_open_media` out of `ctrl_session_run`: `ctx` became a POINTER there, but the call kept `&ctx`. So the TCP video channel received the address of a dead stack slot as its `udata`.
- Machine trace, unambiguous:

  ```
  c9e60:  cbz x0, ...        ; ctx non-null — checked
  c9e64:  ldr x4, [x0]       ; x4 = ctx->p, reads the dead slot
  c9e68:  cbz x4, ...        ; non-null — the safeguard sees nothing
  c9e6c:  ldr x3, [x4, #56]  ; ctx->p->on_video  <-- Data Abort, x4 = 0x70
  ```

  The `!ctx->p` safeguard was powerless: the pointer was not null, it was WRONG. `offsetof(ctrl_session_params, on_video) == 0x38 == 56`, and the faulting address 0xa8 = 0x70 + 56 gives `ctx->p == 0x70`.
- **Why so late**: `:base+20` carries only **key-frame retransmissions**. The callback had never fired in the whole session; it fired for the first time at t=99.3 s and the app went down within the second. The crash looked touch-related — pure coincidence in time.
- The file's three other `&ctx` are inside `ctrl_session_run`, where `ctx` really is a VALUE: correct.
- **Safeguard added**: `session_ctx_t::magic` (`SESSION_CTX_MAGIC`), checked by the four callbacks that receive the context as a `void *` (`vst_to_h264_callback`, `on_video_packet`, `on_cursor_packet`, `on_cursor_full_frame`). `void *` accepts any pointer without a word from the compiler; the sentinel makes the error loud and immediate instead of fatal and late.
- **Method worth keeping**: resolve a crash report against an identically REBUILT `.elf`. The local `.elf` had been rebuilt after the `.nro` was pushed, so the first resolution was suspect. Reconstructing the exact state confirmed the same addresses — and the disassembly settled what `addr2line` alone left in doubt.
- Refs: `streaming/ctrl_session.c::session_open_media`, `core/protocol/ctrl_session_int.h`
- Cross-ref: §7


### 3.33 The stream never stopped — the abort flag hijacked by the trial loop — `[C95+]` RESOLVED
- **Source**: `[S39]` traces placed deliberately, on console, 2026-08-25 (campaign S40).
- **A regression from our automated trial loop.** `np.abort_flag = &fin_essai;` was applied **unconditionally**. With no `autotest.txt` file, `runs` is 1 and `duration` is 0: so the session watched `fin_essai`, which nothing ever raised — the timer that could do so is only created if `duration > 0`. "Leave the stream" therefore could not STRUCTURALLY stop the session.
- Symptom: the UI left the view, the stream kept running underneath (statistics still emitted 55 s after the request), and the user had to open another application to force it closed.
- **Method**: the defect was invisible because NOTHING was logged on that path. Three traces (`exit requested` / `flag raised` / `loop exited`) were enough to locate it in one session: the first two came out, the third never did.
- Fix: in normal use the session watches the application's flag; in a series, `fin_essai` is kept (so that a trial reaching its duration does not look like a "quit") but a watcher thread mirrors it. Verified: the full chain in **234 ms**.
- Corollary fixed along the way: a DELIBERATE exit (`exit_reason == 3`) now returns to the VM list. We used to leave the user on the connection screen, with its seven steps displayed and a "finished" message — a dead end. UNWANTED endings stay displayed: there, you need to know what happened.
- Refs: `clients/borealis/activity/connecting_activity.cpp`, `clients/borealis/activity/stream_activity.cpp`
- Cross-ref: §3.34

### 3.34 The video UDP dies mid-session without the server closing — `[C95+]` LIVE BUG, MEASURED
- **Source**: console, 2026-08-25. Detector D2: `*** VIDEO STALLED *** aucun paquet UDP depuis 2004 ms (t=58s, ctrl_peer_closed=0)`.
- At t=57 s, after 55 s of nominal streaming (~900 packets/s), the video UDP **stops dead**. The control channel stays alive (heartbeats honoured), the server keeps answering our key-frame requests on `:base+20` — but not a single datagram on `:base+10` for 208 s (`rx_age=208065ms`). The picture freezes; the user's only way out is to force a shutdown.
- **It is NOT triggered by a user action**: the first reading concluded there was a triggering click, and it was FALSE — the log accumulated several launches whose timestamps restart at zero, and two sessions had been mixed together. Correctly segmented, the clicks arrive 7 s AFTER the freeze. **Segment by launch (`[0.000] reseau:`) before any temporal correlation.**
- The same family as §3.29 (the silent `:base+30` channel): UDP subscriptions that die or never start while the control channel stays healthy.
- **2026-10-02 — ANSWERED, and the answer is no** `[C90]`: a re-sent `A` datagram CANNOT revive this channel. The server's demux skips a client whose valid byte is clear and matches on the IP, so after an invalidation every datagram is discarded before any dispatcher runs — whatever source port it comes from. The recovery is a re-announcement on `:base+11`; see §9, 2026-10-02 (SRV5).
- **Mitigation (S41)**: D2 could SAY, it did nothing. The cursor channel already had its recovery (AUD5); the video had none. We now resend the UDP registration every 2 s for as long as the video is silent (`SHADOW_VIDEO_REREG=0` to go back). Still to be measured whether the server answers — if not, the cause is deeper.
- Cross-ref: §3.29, §3.31

### 3.35 The server ANNOUNCES why the audio is silent — ~~a second entity holds the channel~~ — `[C90, interpretation SUPERSEDED]`
> **This section's correlation is right; its interpretation is WRONG.**
> The message means "**AUDIO channel down**" and the identifier it carries is a sessionId: these are **OUR** channels, not a second entity's. Decoding and proof in the official binary: **§3.37** for the map, **§3.38** for the cause and the resolution.
> Kept because the 32/32 correlation it documents is what put us on the trail.
- **Source**: a series of 8 instrumented sessions, console, 2026-08-26 (campaign S46). Correlation **8/8, without exception**.
- The server emits small status reports on the control channel (36/41/49 bytes) that we only logged by their SIZE. Decoded:
  - `12 07 7a 05 18 <varint>` = field 15, value **15,000,000**: our announced bitrate, acknowledged.
  - `12 0c 0a 0a 08 05 1a 06 08 <id> 10 <timestamp>` = one report per entity, with an identifier and a last-activity instant. Two entities' values within the same frame differ by **one millisecond**: they are timestamps, not counters.
- **Measured correlation**:

  | server report | audio |
  |---|---|
  | entity `05` ALONE | **present** (3/3) |
  | entity `01` present (alone or with `05`) | **absent** (5/5) |

- Reading: a second entity is registered server-side, and as long as it is, the audio stream does not reach us. That fits exactly condition 2 of the audio campaign (`project_audio_FOUND_cursor_channel.md`): "without a `kUnregisterSession` on close, the server keeps the subscription and the channel stays SILENT on reconnection".
- **It is the first explanation that accounts for EVERYTHING**: the intermittency (~30 %), the binary and immediate character, the byte-identical bootstrap, and the fact that the official client — the only client of its VM — never meets the problem.
- **S48 REFUTED (2026-08-26)**: the official client sends EIGHT deregistration messages on close, one per stream (`f9 { f1 { f1 = stream, f2 = server clock } }`, observed order: with no identifier, then 3, 1, 2, 5, 6, 4, 7), where we sent ONE with an EMPTY body. The gap is real and has been fixed — but a measurement on a clean series (a VM free of any other client) gives **3/10 (30 %)**, exactly the baseline rate. It is not the cause.
- **The correlation holds across 18 consecutive sessions**, without exception, across all series.
- **CORRECTION 2026-08-26 (cross-investigation, 39 agents)**: the claim "sound arrives in groups, hence persistent server state" **DOES NOT SURVIVE MEASUREMENT**. Table rebuilt over 43 post-S36 sessions, 5 launches: runs test p = 0.25; P(sound | previous had sound) = 0.46 against P(sound | previous silent) = 0.40. **No memory from one session to the next.** The `MMMMSSSMMM` pattern it was based on was ONE launch out of five. Stop looking for inter-session state.
- **THREE uncontrolled confounders, found by the same investigation** — every earlier statistic mixes them:
  1. **A real physical gamepad was plugged in and drifting.** It writes ~53 encrypted packets per session on `:base+13` (plug announcements, buttons, axes), and its activity decreases 26 -> 20 -> 13 -> 9 lines per session WITHIN a launch — exactly the profile that produces the supposed "position effect". The official client emits NOTHING of the sort. G45 (§KB) documents that announcing a gamepad switches Windows into gamepad mode, hence a HID enumeration VM-side on every session.
  2. **Three different VMs are mixed** across the series (`gpu-<instance>`, `gpu-<instance>`, `gpu-<instance>`). Only one matches the official capture. A VM effect is indistinguishable from a session effect in that pool.
  3. **`audio2.log` is ONE SINGLE process**: client statics outliving one session into the next have never been tested — while this repo has been caught TWICE by exactly that class (§3.28 and §3.31).
- **THE NOTIFICATION'S ENUM REMAINS UNKNOWN.** One reading proposed `01 = Audio` (enum = field number - 1), resting on `f3.f2 = sessionId`; refuted: `f3.f2` advances by **24,100 ms on every trial**, i.e. exactly the autotest's cadence — it is a clock, not an identifier. The empirical correlation "entity 01 present ⇔ silent" (32/32) HOLDS; its interpretation does not. **Do not rewrite the channel map, and do not move the input from `+14` to `+12` on that basis.**
- **"The official client has sound 100 % of the time" is n = 1**: the capture contains only ONE official session. Yet it is the pillar of the whole investigation. To be measured across 5 launches before drawing anything from it.
- **The defect is NOT an independent draw**: trials 5, 6 and 7 of the clean series had sound THREE TIMES IN A ROW, then nothing. An independent 30 % draw almost never produces such clustering. So there is server-side state that **persists across several sessions and flips** — to be characterised (how long the cluster lasts, what triggers it).
- **Lead for a fix**: make sure the previous session is really released. `GET /clients` (K14) enumerates the server-side clients and `proximus_delete()` already exists; to be cross-checked against the status report to identify entity `01` and delete it before starting.
- Cross-ref: §3.29, §3.31


### 3.36 Gamepad — wire format decoded by guided capture — `[C90]`
- **Source**: guided captures of the official client with a DualShock 4, 2026-08-26 (`captures/scenario_manette_lent_*`), correlated against the local joystick API (`tools/watch_gamepad.py` timestamps every event with its axis number and value).
- **The channel is `:base+13`, and it is the right one**: 1619 packets during gamepad activity against 32 at rest, i.e. ×50. Our client is already emitting in the right place.
- **"Controllers" and "USB" are TWO distinct functions** in the Shadow settings. The gamepad is native; the `shadowusb` package concerns only the forwarding of arbitrary devices (not installed on the test machine). So the note `project_shadow_usbredir_protocol.md`, which says "gamepad tunnel = WSS+Spice+usbredir", is **incomplete**: it describes generic USB forwarding, not the gamepad.
- **Envelope**: a 42-byte UDP packet = a 14-byte payload + nonce 12 + tag 16, the same AEAD as the rest of the protocol.
- **Payload, 14 bytes**:

  ```
  04 00 01 00 | 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 | axis | value | value+0x80
   0  1  2  3 |        4 … 10        |  11  |   12  |     13
  ```

  - bytes 1-2: sub-type. `00 01` = an axis report (93 % of the traffic), `00 02` = a button (byte 3 = a bit mask `01/02/04/08`), `01 03` = the plug announcement, plus `00 00`, `00 04`, `00 05` observed.
  - byte 11: axis index. `2` correlates with local axis 0 (R² = 0.980), `3` with axis 1 (R² = 0.981).
  - byte 12: `round(±axis/258) + 128` — offset binary in one byte; **the Y axis is inverted** (negative slope). 258 = 32767/127.
  - byte 13: the same value in two's complement (`b13 - b12 = 128` in 72 % of cases, ±1 from rounding).
  - Verified across 1626 packets: median deviation 1, 82 % within 2.
- **Our plug announcement is ALREADY correct**: `04 01 03 02 00 …` is exactly what the official client sends (`ctrl_gamepad.c`).
- **BUT the official client does NOT send it when a gamepad is plugged in.** No trace of `/devices`, of Spice, or of the device name in the capture; its UI's "gamepad connected" notification is purely local. Our client, by contrast, announces one every session as soon as it detects a gamepad — traffic the reference never emits, and the main confounder of our measurement series (§3.35).
- Refs: `tools/capture_scenario.sh`, `tools/watch_gamepad.py`, `tools/analyze_scenario.py`
- Cross-ref: §3.25, §3.35

### 3.37 CHANNEL MAP — the official client NAMES it itself — `[C95+]`
- **Source**: the official client's telemetry, in cleartext in our captures of 2026-08-26. It logs `CreateAndConnectSocket` with the channel's name AND its port. Verified on **two different port bases** (10000 and 13000). The server independently declares the same map in its announcement replies (port field = 7000 + offset, across 3 bases).

  ```
  upd: Video       …:10010      tcp: Clipboard   …:10014
  tcp: CtrlChanV2  …:10011      tcp: Cursor      …:10020
  upd: Input       …:10012      upd: AudioOut    …:10030
  upd: Gamepad     …:10013      upd: AudioIn     …:10032
  ```

- **THREE lines of our map were WRONG** and misled the project for months:

  | we believed | it actually is |
  |---|---|
  | `+20` = `VideoSslTcpChannel`, a fallback NAL stream | **`Cursor`** — the pointer's image. 0 Annex-B start codes across 140 frames. |
  | `+30` = the cursor channel, with audio multiplexed on it | **`AudioOut` alone.** The "cursor packets" counted in §3.26/§3.29 are an artefact: `on_cursor_packet()` increments `udp_cursor_pkts` for EVERY packet on the port. |
  | `+14` = the input channel + ComChan | **`Clipboard`**, nothing else. |

- **Immediate consequence, already visible in our logs**: `vst: FRAME byte0=0x12 … nal_len=4132 head: 04000100 00000000 00100000 02000000` — `00 10 00 00` = 4096 bytes of image, `02 00 00 00` = BGRA format. **We have been receiving cursors all along** and the `[RE11] VST frame missing Annex-B start code` guard throws them away.
- `+15`: SFTP over SSH, an `SSH-2.0-libssh` banner in cleartext, outside TLS (**`libssh_0.11.0`**, `password` auth only — no publickey; see §9, 2026-10-02 FT5 for the exact 395-byte form and for why no third-party client can use it). The VM is the SSH **server**; what it puts in the FileTransfer announcement reply is the **SSH password**, not a key the client proves itself with — **never to be logged**, and it is worth more than a channel key: the server does not confine SFTP paths, so that one string is a read/write credential for the VM's whole filesystem. Decoded 2026-10-02, see §3.50.
- `+32`: `AudioIn` (the mic). The official client opens the socket and emits **no bytes at all**; its telemetry says `microphone configuration is: enabled=false`. The identity is proven, the format is not.
- `ComChan` exists in the RTTI and in the official logs but **has no socket**: carried by `+11` or purely internal. Do not go looking for a port for it (cf. D11, which tried to open it on `+14`).
- ~~**Live risk**: `SHADOW_COMCHAN=1` makes us emit a message containing `app_name` every 7 s on `+14`. Since `+14` is the clipboard, **that toggle overwrites the VM's clipboard every 7 seconds**. Do not enable it as it stands.~~ **STRUCK 2026-10-02 (CLIP)**: it cannot, and has not been able to since S52 (2026-08-26) removed opcode 4 from `ctrl_comchan.c`. Only REPLY(4) writes the VM clipboard; the heartbeat sends FLUSH(1) and UPDATE(2), which the server's dispatch **silently ignores** (it has cases for 3 and 4 only), with zero-length bodies. The heartbeat also has its own `SHADOW_COMCHAN_HEARTBEAT`, default 0. `SHADOW_COMCHAN` should still stay off - not because it is dangerous, but because the channel it opens now transfers nothing. The working clipboard is `clip_wire.c` / `clip_chan.c`; adding a REPLY(4) send to `ctrl_comchan.c` instead would re-create the original defect exactly.
- **The audio codec is NEGOTIATED, not fixed**: the same channel, the same official client, Opus on 2026-08-21 and **FLAC** on 2026-08-26 (`audio session granted : Flac - 48000 - 16`). Our code routes any cleartext starting with `0x12` to Opus, and a FLAC frame also starts with `0x12`. And `opus_decode` accepts a badly framed packet and returns NOISE with no error (§3.26). A safeguard is needed.
- **INTEGRATION (2026-08-26, S54/S55)** — four batches, each compiled for both targets and covered:
  1. `streaming/cursor_wire.{c,h}`: the `:base+20` channel's format as a PURE module, verified against real frames (the alpha channel of the 4142-byte frame draws the Windows arrow). 36 checks in `tests/test_cursor_wire.c`, **tested by mutation**: altering the length read, removing a bound, or rejecting the hidden cursor makes the test fail.
  2. `ctrl_video_tcp.c`: framing by the ANNOUNCED length. The parser did "one TCP read = one frame"; a 4142-byte frame was observed split into 1024 + 3118. Type 0x12 frames go to a cursor callback, the others stay on the video path in case the `VideoSslTcpChannel` variant exists.
  3. `stream_view.cpp`: the remote BGRA image is drawn at the position WE track — the server never sends a position. Hotspot aligned, constant size on screen, conversion redone only when the image changes. **Falls back to the synthetic arrow** as long as no image has arrived.
  4. `ctrl_session.c`: nothing from `:base+30` goes to the cursor any more. And `udp_cursor_pkts` now carries the comment that forbids reading it as cursor data — it is that counter, which counts EVERY packet on the audio port, that created the belief in a "cursor channel active 13/20" and skewed hours of measurement.
- **Design choice**: `cursor_state.c` gets a SEPARATE path (`cursor_state_feed_image`) rather than a rewrite. The old `cursor_state_feed()` still reads `:base+30` on the strength of the wrong map; the two states do not tread on each other, each stays testable on its own, and the old one can be removed once the new one has proven itself live.
- Cross-ref: §3.26, §3.29, §3.36


### 3.38 INTERMITTENT AUDIO RESOLVED — we were killing the channel by speaking too early — `[C95+]` RESOLVED
- **Source**: the official binary + 69 instrumented sessions + a validation series of 10, 2026-08-27 (campaigns S56/S57).
- **The server had been announcing the problem all along.** The "41-byte notification" that correlated 32/32 with the absence of sound is decoded. The official binary gives its meaning: `ProcessNotifyMessage` switches on the type, case 5 loads `ctrlchanv2: Channel down: {} (sessionId={})`, and the channel-name table gives the order **VIDEO, AUDIO, INPUT, CURSOR, MIC, CONTROLLER, CLIPBOARD, FILETRANSFER** (indices 0 to 7).
  - `08 01` = **AUDIO CHANNEL DOWN**; `08 05` = CONTROLLER channel down (the plugged-in gamepad confounder).
  - So it was **not** a "phantom entity" nor another client: these are OUR channels. The message had been sleeping in our logs, unreadable for want of a twenty-line decoder.
  - The field taken for a clock (`g_srv_clock_ms`) is a **sessionId** — the binary prints it under that name.
- **THE CAUSE**: `t_last_cursor_ping_ms` and `t_last_cursor_reg_ms` were 0. Since `now_ms` is a monotonic clock in milliseconds, `now_ms - 0 >= 7500` was true **from the very first iteration**: our `0x70` ping (AUD4) and our registration resend (AUD5) both went out ~200 ms after the channel was registered.
- **That is exactly the window in which the first audio frame is in flight**: it arrives 201 to 227 ms after registration, our sends land between 197 and 244 ms. The first 7 frames measured fall INSIDE that window. The official client emits NOTHING between its registration and its first frame (first ping at +7.495 s, no re-registration).
- **Correlation across 69 sessions, total separation on one side**:

  | | AUD5 fired | AUD5 silent |
  |---|---|---|
  | with sound | **0** | 21 |
  | without sound | 43 | 5 |

  and the server answers `CHANNEL_DOWN(AUDIO)` 40 to 130 ms later.
- **AUD5 had been added to RESCUE a channel that does not start; it was the thing preventing it from starting.** Seven server-side hypotheses were tested and refuted before anyone looked at our own emission schedule.
- **Fix (S57)**: both counters are armed at the moment the loop starts — first ping at +7.5 s like the official client, first resend at +2 s. `SHADOW_AUD_QUIET=0` restores the old behaviour.
- **VALIDATED**: a series of 10 sessions, **10 out of 10 with sound (100 %)** against 21 out of 69 (30 %) before, and **zero `CHANNEL_DOWN(AUDIO)`**. P(10/10 | p=0.3) < 10^-5.
- **Oracle (S56)**: `g_audio_channel_down` is raised as soon as the server announces the channel's death, i.e. ~1 s into the session instead of 15 s of silence to interpret. Deliberately with no log cap — it is capping by `static` that made failures invisible from the 2nd session on (§3.28).
- **Lesson**: an undecoded protocol message is lost information. This one cost seven hypotheses, two nights, and dozens of measurement series.
- **2026-09-11 — both recovery emitters bounded, and the S57 signature logged (AUD-LC-4)** `[C95+]` for the code; `[C60]` for the effect in service (no `CHANNEL_DOWN(AUDIO)` observed since S57). What remained after S57: AUD5 resent the registration every 2 s ENDLESSLY on a channel that delivers nothing (at the bench, 900 sends in 30 min, 807 of them with no line) — capped at 12 (`SHADOW_AUD5_MAX`, `0` = off like the official client), every resend logged; AUD16 promised 12 attempts per session but its counter went back to 0 on every REVIVAL (174 sends in 30 min with one stray datagram per minute, 124 with the 11 B beacon every 7 s, 123 of them towards a socket fed less than 10 s earlier — this paragraph's configuration) — session budget `SHADOW_AUDIO_REVIVE_MAX` (12). And this paragraph's signature, `CHANNEL_DOWN(AUDIO)` 40 to 130 ms after OUR emission, was measured by nothing: `[AUD18] CHANNEL_DOWN(AUDIO) a t=…s, … ms apres notre derniere emission :base+30 (ping|AUD5|AUD16|S43 #n)` now dates it on every announcement, and `[AUD5] premier paquet :base+30 apres N reenvoi(s)` finally says whether a resend ever brings a channel back. No emission added; first ping at +7.5 s and first resend at +2 s unchanged. See §9, AUD-LC-4.
- **2026-10-02 — WHY THE RESENDS NEVER WORKED** `[C90]`: not a tuning problem. Once the server has invalidated the stream client, the channel's demux discards our datagrams unread, so AUD5 and AUD16 were speaking into a socket nobody was reading — "807 of 900 sends with no line" is the demux, not the engine. A single `'G'` (0x47) byte is the in-protocol way to ask for the codec header again when the channel IS alive, and a re-announcement is the only way back when it is not. See §9, 2026-10-02 (SRV4/SRV5).
- Cross-ref: §3.26, §3.29, §3.35, §3.37

## 4. Gamepad — the `shadow-controller` DC via a `GamepadChannel` FlatBuffer

> **PIVOT 2026-05-03 PM**: the Spice/usbredir lead is abandoned (cf. §2.5). The client simply uses the `shadow-controller` data channel (length-prefixed FlatBuffer).

### 4.0 Confirmed architecture — `[C95]`
**Source**: strings from `06-shadow-recon-linux/ShadowPCDisplay` (the Linux Shadow desktop binary) + a **live capture `captures/shadow-session9.json`** (a DS4 forwarded, 1234 sends on shadow-controller)

**Wire format observed**: the same framing as shadow-input (`[size:4 LE][24:4 LE][zeros:8][0e 00]` + FlatBuffer).

| Type | Vtable signature (bytes 16..31) | Size | Count (52 s) | Model |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Announce | `1c 00 1b 00 00 00 0c 00 0b 00 04 00 0e 00 00 00` | 136 B | 1× at start-up | `GamepadPluggedInputV4Model` |
| Input | `20 00 1f 00 14 00 0c 00 0b 00 04 00 0e 00 00 00` | 136 B | 1207× (~25 Hz) | `GamepadAxis/ButtonInputV4Model` |
| Ping | `24 00 23 00 14 00 0c 00 0b 00 04 00 0e 00 00 00` | 128 B | 23× (~0.5 Hz) | `GamepadPingInputV4Model` |

**Field encoding** (confirmed by correlating the DOM gamepad-state against the wire in session9):
- byte 48: sequence number, uint8 (monotonic, +1 per send) — as in shadow-input
- bytes 32-47: the root table (table_size + uoffsets to the sub-tables and the timestamp)
- byte 127: the sub-table's type discriminator (`04` left stick, `06` right stick or button, others TBD)
- bytes 128-135: **variable data**:
  - **Left stick LX/LY (axis 0/1)**: an s16 LE value in **bytes 132-133**, bytes 134-135 = `00 02`/`00 03`
  - **Right stick RX/RY (axis 2/3)**: an s16 LE value in **bytes 134-135**, bytes 132-133 = `00 00`
  - **Y axes inverted** on the wire (DOM Y+ = downwards → wire Y+ = upwards)
  - Axis encoding: `wire_value = round(dom_value x 32767)`, sign inverted for Y

Confirmed examples:
- `axis0=+0.520` → wire `08 00 00 00 84 42 00 02` (0x4284=17028=0.520×32767 ✓)
- `axis2=-0.150` → wire `08 00 00 00 00 00 d9 ec` (0xecd9=-4903=-0.150×32767 ✓)

**DS4 button mapping → bytes 119/124/126/132-135** (session10 capture, 18 isolated actions):

| DS4 button | byte 119 | byte 124 | byte 126 | bytes 132-135 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cross/Circle/Square/Options/Share/L1/R1 | 03 | 07 | 04 | 00 00 00 03 |
| Triangle | 02 | 07 | 06 | 00 00 01 10 |
| D-pad Down | 02 | 07 | 06 | 00 00 01 01 |
| D-pad Up | 02 | 00 | 07 | 00 00 00 01 |
| D-pad Left | 02 | 07 | 06 | 00 00 01 02 |
| D-pad Right (with stray stick motion) | 03 | 07 | 04 | f7 fb 00 02 |
| L2 trigger (analogue 0..255) | 04 | 00 | 07 | 00 00 00 7e |
| R2 trigger | 04 | 07 | 06 | 00 00 56 01 |
| L3/R3 (stick clicks) | 03 | 07 | 04 | (axis values + 00 02 / 00 01) |

The exact FlatBuffer button-index mapping inside the sub-vtable is not decoded yet (complex FB indirections). To get started on the Switch we can **replay each DS4 pattern byte-perfect** according to the mapped Joy-Con input.

**1 send = 1 axis OR 1 button update** (the DOM gamepad-state holds the complete state; the browser emits ~3 separate sends when a stick moves → one per changed axis).

Decoding the sub-vtables exactly (button index, hat direction, V4 plugged fields) requires Ghidra RE of the serialisation functions `GamepadAxisInputV4Model::Pack` and so on. For now we have enough to start a **replay/PoC** on the Switch (capture an observed Plugged, send it and adapt bytes 128-135 to the Joy-Con).



The `GamepadChannel`'s C++ FlatBuffer classes:

| Class | Role |
|---|---|
| **`GamepadPluggedInputV4Model`** | **Discovery**: announces that a gamepad is plugged in → the VM creates a virtual Xbox 360 (ViGEm) |
| `GamepadUnpluggedInputV4Model` | Announces a disconnection |
| `GamepadButtonInputV4Model` | A button press/release |
| `GamepadAxisInputV4Model` | A stick/trigger value |
| `GamepadHatInputV4Model` | D-pad/hat |
| `GamepadPingInputV4Model` | Keepalive |
| `GamepadTrackingInputVrModel`, `GamepadFovVrModel`, `GamepadIpdVrModel` | VR variants (unused here) |
| `GamepadModel` | Base |

**Key insight**: there is **no USB discovery at all**. It is a simple `Plugged` FlatBuffer message at start-up that informs the VM. The VM then sees a local virtual Xbox 360. So our Switch must:
1. At session start → send `GamepadPluggedInputV4Model` (as if a gamepad had just been plugged in)
2. On every Joy-Con update → send `GamepadButtonInputV4Model` / `GamepadAxisInputV4Model` / `GamepadHatInputV4Model`
3. On exit → send `GamepadUnpluggedInputV4Model`

### 4.1 Implementation plan
1. Open the `shadow-controller` DC on the Switch side (DCEP already handled in M15)
2. Decode each V4Model's FlatBuffer vtable through Ghidra on ShadowPCDisplay
3. Map the Joy-Con (`padGetButtons`, `padGetStickPos`) → standard Xbox 360 format (ViGEm mapping)
4. Send through the `shadow-controller` DC

### 4.2 ShadowUSB on Mac (= a distinct case, not our target) — `[C95]`
The Mac ShadowUSB daemon exists for a separate case: an exotic USB device that cannot be mapped to an Xbox 360 (a Logitech wheel, a HOTAS) and is tunnelled as raw USB over Spice. For Joy-Con → Xbox 360 it is pointless. See §2.5 for why that endpoint is inaccessible to us.



### 4.1 Mac ShadowUSB daemon architecture (RE from strings) — `[C95]`
**Source**: the `ShadowUSB` daemon extracted from Shadow's Mac package - Mach-O x86_64, v4.4.5

Stack confirmed by demangled C++ symbols:
```
TCP+TLS (port 443)
  └── WebSocket (Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13, Upgrade negotiation)
       └── SpiceLink (magic "REDQ" + capabilities)
            └── Auth via SpiceLinkAuthMechanism + SpiceLinkEncryptedTicket
                 └── SpiceMainChannel
                      └── SpiceUsbRedirChannel (sub-channel)
                           └── standard usbredir frames
```

C++ classes identified:
- `SslWebsocketSpiceChannel<SpiceMainChannel<SslWebsocketClientChannel>>`
- `SpiceUsbRedirChannel`, `SpiceUsbDeviceForwarder`, `SpiceUsbRedirChannelWrapper`
- `WebsocketClientChannel`, `WebsocketFrame`

Daemon plist: `/Library/ShadowUSB/shadowusb_run.sh --ws --filter` (`--ws` = WebSocket mode active)

Bundled libs: libusbredirhost, libusbredirparser, libcurl, libmsquic.2.4.5, libusb-1.0
- Refs: `memory/project_shadow_usbredir_protocol.md`

### 4.2 Daemon JSON templates (Ghidra, Mach-O strings) — `[C95]`
**Source**: the same `ShadowUSB` daemon - its string table around index 34985-35220, plus the Ghidra cross-reference dump

Templates found in cleartext in the binary:
```
{ "data": { "id": "", "tlskey": "" } }                    <- response to /clients type=usb (confirmed)
{ "data": { "id": "", "spice_url": "" } }                 <- response to /devices, Spice mode
{ "data": { "id": "", "port": 0 } }                        <- response to /devices, XHCI mode (= what we get)
{ "error": { "message": "" } }                             <- error
{ "protocol": "", "type": "", "url": "" }                  <- general BODY template for POST /devices
{ "protocol": "spice", "type": "spice-html5" }             <- BODY for POST /devices, Spice mode (2 fields)
```

Conclusion: there are 2 modes for `/N/devices`:
- **XHCI mode**: body `{"protocol":"XHCI"}` → response `{id, port}` (port = not an external TCP one)
- **Spice mode**: body `{"protocol":"spice","type":"spice-html5"}` → response `{id, spice_url}` (a ready-made WSS URL)

### 4.3 Critical bug: X-Shadow-Agent missing on `http_post_json` — `[C95]` LIVE BUG (fixed 2026-05-03 PM)
**Source**: Ghidra RE of the `X-Shadow-Agent` string in the daemon + a diff against our Switch code

- Our `shadow/http.c::http_post_json` did NOT send the `X-Shadow-Agent` header
- Consequence: `POST /N/devices` with a Spice body → 400 "Invalid parameters" while an XHCI body gets through (probably because XHCI is the less strict legacy path)
- Fix: the header added to `http_post_json` (commit 2026-05-03 PM)
- To retest: if the Spice body now gets through, we retrieve `spice_url` directly

### 4.4 Spice token refresh — a separate endpoint — `[C90]`
**Source**: `wss_xrefs.txt`, `FUN_100174d90` xref to "Refresh Spice token"
- In `vmproxy_http_channel.cpp::refreshSpiceToken`
- Parses the response's `"url"` field
- The exact endpoint still to be RE'd — probably `/N/devices/<id>/refresh` or `/N/spice/refresh`
- Use case: the Spice token expires during the session and has to be renewed

### 4.5 Mac daemon stack (RE from strings + C++ symbols) — `[C95]`
```
TCP+TLS (port 443)
  └── WebSocket (Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13, Upgrade negotiation)
       └── SpiceLink (magic "REDQ" + capabilities)
            └── Auth via SpiceLinkAuthMechanism + SpiceLinkEncryptedTicket
                 └── SpiceMainChannel
                      └── SpiceUsbRedirChannel (sub-channel)
                           └── standard usbredir frames
```

Demangled C++ classes: `SslWebsocketSpiceChannel<SpiceMainChannel<SslWebsocketClientChannel>>`, `SpiceUsbRedirChannel`, `SpiceUsbDeviceForwarder`, `SpiceUsbRedirChannelWrapper`, `WebsocketClientChannel`.

Daemon plist: `/Library/ShadowUSB/shadowusb_run.sh --ws --filter`. Libs: libusbredirhost, libusbredirparser, libcurl, libmsquic.2.4.5, libusb-1.0.

## 7. Switch HOS quirks

### 7.1 Force IPv4 — `[C95]`
- Strip the `ipv6-` prefix → resolve `ipv4-...` only
- Refs: `demo/src/streaming/shadowusb.c::strip_ipv6_prefix`

### 7.2 TLS on external threads — `[C95]`
- `static __thread` crashes on threads created by libjuice (TLS not initialised)
- Stack-allocate everywhere in callbacks from external code
- Refs: `memory/feedback_switch_thread_local_storage.md`

### 7.3 Thread lifecycle — `[C95]`
- Every long-running thread must poll `abort_flag` at <=100 ms granularity
- Otherwise `process_exit` kills it brutally and HOS leaks (a reboot is then mandatory)
- Refs: `memory/feedback_switch_thread_lifecycle.md`

### 7.4 Degraded HOS / curl_global_init — `[C95]`
- A `curl_global_init` that fails cleanly = reboot the console, do not go hunting for a code bug
- Refs: `memory/feedback_switch_hos_degraded.md`

### 7.5 `wolfssl/options.h` breaks pthread — `[C95]`
- **Source**: the Switch build of 2026-08-21, `third_party/wolfssl/build_switch/wolfssl/options.h:38`
- `options.h` does `#undef _POSIX_THREADS`. Newlib gates half of `pthread.h` behind that macro: `pthread_create`, `pthread_join`, all the `pthread_mutex_*`. The `pthread_rwlock_*` stay visible (a different macro), hence a misleading error message that suggests `pthread_rwlock_unlock` in place of `pthread_mutex_unlock`.
- **Rule**: in any `.c` that uses pthread, `#include <pthread.h>` BEFORE the first wolfSSL header — including a local header that includes one (`ctrl_audio_dtls.h` includes `wolfssl/ssl.h`).
- The Linux build sees nothing: glibc does not gate its declarations. The symptom only shows up on the Switch build, potentially sessions later.
- Refs: `core/protocol/ctrl_input_tcp.c` (the file's header), `CMakeLists.txt` (`-D_POSIX_THREADS=1`, insufficient on its own)

### 3.39 Gamepad — announce ONCE, or you get phantom pads — `[C90]`
- **Source**: live observation 2026-08-27 (Steam shows 4 gamepads), code `core/protocol/ctrl_gamepad.c`.
- The gamepad has been **recognised end to end** since G48 (axis scale read through `EVIOCGABS`) and S58/S59/S60 (channel wake-up). Validated against a gamepad test site.
- The plug announcement (`04 01 03 02` + zeros, 14 B on `:base+13`) creates a gamepad on the VM side. **Each announcement creates a NEW one** — it does not refresh the existing one.
- P6 (2026-08-22) repeated the announcement at 1 s / 3 s / 6 s. Four announcements in total = **4 gamepads**, which matches the observation exactly. P6 is therefore REFUTED; its founding hypothesis ("the server has not finished opening the channel") was in fact compensating for the real causes, fixed since.
- **Important diagnostic consequence**: a gamepad test site enumerates EVERY device and reacts to any of them — so the pad looks perfect there even with phantoms around. A GAME binds to ONE device: if it picks a phantom instance it receives nothing more, while the client is still sending. "Works on the test site, dead in the game" is the signature of this defect, not of an encoding problem.
- Default: `SHADOW_GAMEPAD_REPLUG=0`. `=1` restores the reminder. The pause menu keeps the manual "re-announce the gamepad" action for when it really is lost.
- Refs: `streaming/ctrl_gamepad.c::ctrl_gamepad_replug_tick`, §3.20
- Cross-ref: §3.20 (input encoding), §9

### 3.49 The announcement replies — DECODED, and they carried a private key into our logs — `[C95+]` RESOLVED
- **Source**: 170 replies across 30 capture sets, six different port bases, cross-checked against 49 lines of the official client's telemetry. Serialiser confirmed in the binary (`sub_10E5E00`).
- **LEAK FIXED, and it was live**: `ctrl_session.c` logged the first 160 bytes of EVERY reply, in hex AND in ASCII. The 8th — FileTransfer — carries an **OpenSSH ed25519 private key in cleartext** starting at offset 33 of the body. Every complete bootstrap therefore wrote a live key into `shadow-client.log`, which `tools/switch-logsink.sh` then copied **in cleartext over TCP** to the dev machine. Replaced by `ann_reply.c`, which reads the useful fields and **never copies** the key field; the `test_ann_reply.c` suite looks for a marker anywhere in the output structure, so adding a field that reads it makes the test fail.
- **Envelope** (identical for every control reply): `[u16be type][u16be len][protobuf]`, `type == 1` in 6310/6310 — it is not a discriminant. The server sends the 4-byte header in ITS OWN TLS record, then the body in a second one: that is why a "one read = one message" reader loses alignment here.
  - `f1` sequence number, `f3` reply payload, `f4` server identity, `f5` client identity echoed back, `f2` unsolicited event.
- **`f3.8` = ChannelInfo, and EXACTLY ONE field is set in it: ITS NUMBER IS THE CHANNEL** — 1 Video, 2 Audio, 3 Input, 4 Cursor, 5 Mic, 6 Gamepad, 7 Clipboard, 8 FileTransfer. So the channel does **not** depend on arrival order, contrary to what our comment assumed.
- **Common body**: `f1` SessionInfo { `f2` = granted session handle (a server clock in ms), `f4` StreamingProtocol { `f1` protocol (0 SUFP, 4 FlatBuffers, 5 SFTP), `f3` network (0 UDP, 1 TCP), `f4` encrypted = 1 in 170/170, `f5` port } }.
- **THE TRAP**: `f5` was 7010/7012/7013/7014/7015/7020/7030/7032 in **170/170** replies, across six different port bases. That is the port INTERNAL to the VM, invariant — dialling it connects to nothing. What it does give, from the server, on every session, is `f5 % 1000` = **the channel's OFFSET**. The §3.37 table is therefore confirmed by the server on every round.
- **Per-channel extras**: VIDEO `f2` = granted mode {width, height, f32 frame rate}, `f3` codec (absent = H.264, 1 = H.265), `f5` 4:4:4, `f7` granted bitrate ceiling. AUDIO/MIC `f2` sample rate, `f3` bits, `f4` **granted codec** (1 Opus, 2 FLAC). INPUT `f2` = {10,1,6,10}. CURSOR/GAMEPAD/CLIPBOARD: SessionInfo alone.
- **WHAT THIS CLOSES**: the **granted** video mode (§3.8b, open since L18) and the **granted** audio codec — until now we assumed our request had been honoured. Cross-ref: §3.8b, §3.37, §3.48

### 3.47 Clipboard `:base+14` — the wire, established from the binary — `[C95+]` RESOLVED
> **The field layout announced here on 2026-09-02 was WRONG**, and it still produced the right numbers. Kept at the end of the section, because the reason for the error is the real lesson.

- **Source**: the official client itself, verified with objdump.
  ```
  headerSize() @0xabd440 : mov eax,0xa ; ret                -> 10 bytes, fixed
