Three additions, all of them reading measurements that already existed.
  **In the pause menu's panel, network section**: loss rate (a gauge, thresholds 0.3 % and 1 % taken from measurement), chunks of frames never opened, truncated frames, packets dropped by the system, round trip to the VM with mean, p90 and jitter. Lines that are permanently zero (kernel, truncated frames) are shown ONLY if they are non-zero: a line that is always zero is a line you stop reading.
  **A dedicated page** (`ui/net_test.{hpp,cpp}`), shared by Settings and the pause menu like the gamepad and mouse testers. It gives what the panel cannot: what the link CARRIES, accumulated over the session, via `path_probe.h`.
  **Sampling runs with the page CLOSED**, inside `draw` and outside any display guard. That was the point not to miss: an estimate that only built itself when the page was open would never have enough samples — and it would have been the fifth time this repo only measured what it was looking at.
  **WHAT THE PAGE REFUSES TO SAY**: a single number. Below 12 samples of which 3 with loss, it shows "not enough to conclude yet" rather than a number that would look just as confident. With no loss, it says "no ceiling found UP TO X" and not "no ceiling" — the second wording invites you to push higher. And a permanent note recalls that the round trip is measured over TCP on the control channel, not on the UDP video path, and that the loss rate is a LOWER BOUND.
  Validation measurement, desktop: 40 round trips, mean 20 ms, p90 23 ms, jitter 1 ms.

- **2026-09-03 (S118)** — **THE CLIENT HAD NO NETWORK-LATENCY MEASUREMENT AT ALL. IT HAS ONE NOW, AND IT COSTS NOTHING.** `[C95+]` The per-stage report counts TWELVE measurements and **eleven of them are local** — decode queue, decode, upload, draw rate. The twelfth, the spread of a frame's UDP burst, measures JITTER and loss, **never propagation delay**: a link at a constant 200 ms read exactly like a link at 5 ms. So this client could not answer "what is my latency to the VM", which is the first question you ask in game.
  **The measurement had always been in the protocol**: the `:base+11` control channel is request/reply and **the server echoes the request's sequence number** in field 1 (established across 170 replies, §3.49). We already emit a heartbeat there every 500 ms. All it took was timestamping the send and matching the reply — **no traffic added**, which matters here: this repo is byte-exact by discipline and emitting what the server does not expect can kill a channel (S57).
  **Desktop measurement, the first of its kind**: 21 ms, jitter 0, matched every 500 ms. The bounded diagnostic shows the mechanics: `seq=11` and `seq=12` are the last two announcements (predating the loop, hence unmatched), then `seq=13, 14, 15...` each match a `f3.3` reply.
  **WHAT THIS NUMBER IS NOT, and the display must say so**: an **application-level TCP+TLS** round trip to the VM. It is NOT the latency of the video path, which is UDP and can behave differently — different queuing and prioritisation. Writing plain "latency" would promise the other one.
  `core/protocol/rtt.h`, a pure module, 36 checks. Its counter-cases are refusals: an unsolicited server message does not become a zero round trip, a duplicated reply does not produce a second, longer one, and a clock that goes backwards does not become an enormous duration after conversion to unsigned — the L21 family.

- **2026-09-03 (S117)** — **THE CLIENT STOPPED MEASURING ITS OWN BITRATE AS SOON AS THE PANEL WAS CLOSED.** `[C95+]` The fourth instance of the family CLAUDE.md names first, and the most expensive so far: the seven rate counters — including `hud_live_kbps_` and `rate_bytes_` — lived INSIDE `if ((show_perf_stats || hud_.editing()) && hud_.visible())` (`clients/borealis/activity/stream_view.cpp:1618`). So the received bitrate only existed while you were looking at it. The previous three: `stat_latency` fed only with the panel open (fixed by L5), `buf_drop_count` absent from every public structure, and the loss counters written but unreadable (below). Sampling moves out of the guard; only the drawing and the RENDER rate gap, which only means anything for drawing, stay inside.
  **AND LOSS WAS WRITTEN WITHOUT BEING READABLE.** `chunks_missing`, `chunks_expected`, `chunks_orphan_lost`, `frames_dropped_trunc` and the `SO_RXQ_OVFL` counter lived in `ctrl_session_stats` and reached the PUBLIC structure by no path at all: the panel carried only frame rates. A user whose picture degraded while playing could not see their own loss — they had to quit, pull the log over FTP and read a stats line. **That is exactly what happened this evening.** All five counters plus the forward clock offset are now published every 250 ms.
  **A WARNING ABOUT THE FIGURE**, and it applies to every past reading: `chunks_missing / chunks_expected` is a **LOWER BOUND**. A frame whose opening chunk is lost is never opened, so it enters neither counter (`vid_reasm.c`, the `return` that precedes all accounting); its chunks land in `chunks_orphan_lost`, published alongside for exactly that reason. The "1.66 %" recorded in the console session of 2026-09-03 is therefore a floor.

- **2026-09-03 (S117b)** — **THE PATH'S CAPACITY CAN BE READ FROM THE STREAM, WITHOUT EMITTING ANYTHING SPECIAL.** `[C90]` `core/protocol/path_probe.h`, a pure module, 25 offline checks. The official launcher measures the link with its own traffic; **we cannot copy it** — no capture contains an endpoint of that kind (the official client's only REST paths are `/4/clients`, `/4/status`, `/4/stream`, `/4/version`, `/4/stats`), and the binary's `ShadowBench` is a **local video-decode** bench, not a network test. Inventing traffic would in any case go against the byte-exact discipline and the S57 risk.
  **The stream IS the measurement**, and that is better: it runs with the real protocol, over UDP, in bursts, against the real server. The server regulates — it climbs, loses, backs off, climbs again — so the knee reads from crossing bitrate against loss. Reference measurement on console: 16.2 Mb/s mean in the samples with loss against 14.4 without, and a 3.6 Mb/s back-off after each loss.
  **WHAT THE MODULE REFUSES TO DO**: return a single number. A link does not have ONE bitrate. It publishes the highest bitrate seen clean, the lowest seen with loss, the sample count — and **it advises nothing** until it has 12 samples of which 3 with loss. With no loss at all, the verdict is not "no ceiling at 20" but "no ceiling found up to 20": the first wording invites you to push higher, the second says what is known. Zero-bitrate samples are ignored, without which a paused session would produce a ceiling of zero.

- **2026-09-03 (DEBIT-1)** — **ABOVE ~25 Mb/s THE PICTURE DEGRADES IN GAME ON CONSOLE, AND THE DESKTOP BENCH IS STRUCTURALLY BLIND TO THIS DEFECT.** `[C80]`
  **The symptom, reported live**: console docked + Ethernet, in game, `bitrate_eth_mbps = 100`. The server grants 50 Mb/s, the user receives ~30 at 90 fps with a render loop at 100/s, and sees "like the previous picture" — regular artefacts where the previous frame persists. **The user's own A/B: switched to 25 Mb/s, the artefacts disappear.** That is the only measurement we have of the faulty regime, and it is worth one point, not a curve.
  **THE DESKTOP BENCH CANNOT MEASURE IT, and that is the real lesson.** Ladder of 25 / 40 / 60 / 80 / 100 Mb/s on `shadow-test-cli`, wired desktop, 90 s each: the REAL bitrate stays between **6.5 and 7.4 Mb/s at every rung**, perfect integrity throughout (decryption 100.0 %, slices 50.0 %, ~60 fps). With `--activity`: 6.3 Mb/s. **The encoder produces what the CONTENT demands** — a motionless desktop, even with synthetic activity, never asks for 30 Mb/s. Asking for 100 therefore changes nothing about what arrives, and the bench declares "all is well" at precisely the rung where the console breaks. It is `CLAUDE.md`'s warning about bad proxies, applied to bitrate: the G4 defect was already invisible on a static desktop.
  **WHAT IS NOT ESTABLISHED**: exactly where the breaking point lies. We know 100 breaks and 25 holds, in game, on console, over Ethernet. Nothing in between has been tried, and the ceiling granted by the server (50 in this session, 70 in captures) is yet a third number.
  **WHAT IS NOT THE CAUSE**: the setting is not new. Before B1's unification on 2026-09-03, the per-link ladder already went to 100 and the Quality screen went up to **150** — B1 LOWERED the ceiling to 100. So the defect is old and simply had never been met, for want of having played at that bitrate.
  **Pending the measurement**: the descriptions of all three bitrate settings carry the warning, with the date and the two measured values, rather than an invented ceiling. **The experiment that would settle it**: the same game session at 40, 50, 60 Mb/s on console, noting when the artefacts appear — it requires playing, no bench replaces it.

- **2026-09-03 (S41d)** — **THE VIDEO OUTAGE DOES NOT REPRODUCE ON A WIRED DESKTOP: 0 IN 1787 s AGAINST ONE EVERY 22 s ON CONSOLE.** `[C95+]` The control run `CLAUDE.md` has been asking for since 2026-08-29, done at last — through the CLI client rather than a cable on the console, but done.
  **The record**: six runs of 298 s on `shadow-test-cli`, wired Linux desktop, sound off. 1787 s of stream, 71,511 frames. **Zero outages** in the sense of the D4 detector (300 ms threshold). The six runs have a regularity that is itself a reference: `fps` 39.8 to 40.3 (range 0.5), **decryption 100.0 % in all six**, **slice ratio exactly 50.0 % in all six**, 5.08 to 5.13 Mbit/s.
  **The contrast**: `captures/tests_autonomes_20260828/switch_D4_wifi.log`, a single launch, 1523 s, **69 outages** — one every 22 s. At the same rate, the desktop campaign should have produced **81**. It produced zero; the probability of that result under the console's rate is of the order of `e^-81`. **These are not two measurements of the same phenomenon.**
  **WHAT IT DOES NOT ESTABLISH, and this must be said**: the desktop differs from the console in TWO ways at once — the link (wired against Wi-Fi) **and** the platform (x86/Linux against HOS/Switch, different network stack and decode path). So the result rules out "a defect in our session code, independent of the medium" — since it is the SAME `ctrl_session.c` running on both sides — and narrows the cause down to **the console and/or wireless**. It does not choose between the two.
  **The experiment that settles it, and it is the same one as on 29 August**: the console, **wired** (dock + Ethernet adapter). It changes only the link and holds the platform constant. We now have the control arm to compare it against.
  **Useful side effect**: the S41b instrument (SUFP timestamp delta at recovery) could say nothing — there was no recovery to measure. It is waiting for a session that cuts out, hence the console.

- **2026-09-03 (S41 MEASURED)** — **THE S41 RECOVERY DOES NOTHING, AND THE CLIENT REQUESTED NO KEY FRAME AT ALL DURING A SILENCE.** `[C95+]` Two findings, one single record.
  1. **S41 is inert.** The registration resend fired ~134 times during the instrumented session's outages and **the server never replied**: the two long outages absorbed ~67 and ~47 resends (one every 2.000 s for 143.9 s and 99.2 s), zero packets in return. Across the 20 outages with at least one resend, the delay between the last resend and recovery spreads across the whole 2 s window (median 705 ms) — **the two events are independent**. The packet is nonetheless byte-exact; what is missing is the CONTEXT. When the official client ends up there, it **redoes the whole bootstrap** (new ClientHello on `:base+11`, Capabilities → Authentication → Encryption → DisplayConfig → RegisterSession → the eight announcements) then registers `:base+10` **and** `:base+30` within the same millisecond; the video comes back 60 ms later. We were sending step 12 of 12. **Moved to 0 by default.**
  2. **No key-frame request during the silence.** Census of `g_idr_needed`'s triggers: three are inside packet handling, G13 requires fifteen frames DELIVERED to the decoder, G34 a frame dropped from the queue, G18 a bitstream error (at 0 since G37). **All of them require something to arrive** — so when nothing arrives, the client asks for nothing. Verified: not a single `[G13]` or `[G18]` line during the two outages. It is word for word the defect CLAUDE.md names first, for audio (S57). **And the request did not go where it should**: over UDP, `iP` goes out on `udp_video`, the very socket that is silent; the only path valid on both transports is `Request_Flush` on `:base+11`, a channel that is **alive** during the outage (304 and 209 control frames exchanged during the two silences). Fixed by `SHADOW_IDR_ON_STALL`, one request / 3 s, ceiling 8.
  **The silence is NOT a link failure**: during the 143.9 s, `:base+30` delivered 200.0 packets/s of REAL audio, `:base+11` exchanged every ~500 ms, the server echoed our clicks back, and `noyau=0` (no buffer overflow). Link, socket layer, receive loop and host were all alive. **This is video-specific.** And the shape is not a revoked subscription but a rate regulator: 1855 packets/s at t=494, 32 at t=504, 0 from t=604; on recovery 838 → 1450 → 1586 → 1690 → 1833 → 1880 over ~30 s.
  **The official client does not know this failure**: 26 UDP sessions, 2529.6 s, 524,302 packets on `:base+10`, maximum inter-arrival gap 408 ms in 25 of them. The one gap beyond a second (7.2 s) is explained in its own telemetry by a `VmProxy timeout` followed by a deliberate stop of the stream.
  **WHAT REMAINS TO BE SETTLED, and the instrument is in place (S41b)**: did the encoder run during the silence? The `[D4] video RETABLIE` line now publishes the SUFP timestamp delta (`g_last_frame_id`, bytes 6-9) between the last packet before and the first after. A delta on the scale of the outage = datagrams lost in transit, and the whole discussion above becomes moot; a delta near zero = the server produced nothing. **The same measurement settles that field's unit** (microseconds or a frame counter: four orders of magnitude apart), an open question in its own right.

- **2026-09-03 (RE-5)** — **THE CLIPBOARD WIRE WAS SPLIT WRONG, AND IT PRODUCED THE RIGHT NUMBERS.** `[C95+]` We were writing `[u32 type][u32 sub][u16 length]`. The binary says four fields: `[u16_be reserved][u16_be opcode][u16_be format][u32_be length]`. There is no `sub` — those bytes are the format and the **high half of the length** — and `reserved` is a **guard** (`bodySize()` @0xabd460 returns -1 if the first two bytes are non-zero).
  **Why nobody saw it**: `reserved` and `format` are 0 today, so the `u32 type` read the correct opcode, and the `u16` at offset 8 reads the **low half** of the real u32, which IS the whole value below 65,536. Measured: the two encodings are identical byte for byte below 64 KiB. The break is therefore at **65,536**, not at 256 — an earlier note said so, wrong by a factor of 256. A decoding that yields the right numbers on the available data is not a correct decoding — here three fields out of four were zero, and it was the zero that hid the error.
  **The names too**: `1` = Flush and `2` = Update, not FOCUS_CHANGED and WINDOW_STATE_CHANGED; `3` = Request (which RE-1 had found without naming it); `4` = ReplyText, the content. The "focus triplet" was "drop what you have / I have TEXT / here it is".
  **And the point that decides the implementation**: the server -> client direction is a **PULL**. The server announces `Update`, and it is up to the CLIENT to send `Request` to get the text. A client that merely listens will never receive anything. §3.47

- **2026-09-03 (VERROU-1)** — **KEY DERIVATION MEASURED ON CONSOLE: 553 ms FOR 60,000 ROUNDS.** `[C95+]` Against 55 ms for the same count on an x86_64 laptop — a factor of 10, which is what a Cortex-A57 at 1020 MHz predicts. Recorded in the console's log: `[verrou] ouvert — derivation 553 ms (60000 tours)`.
  **The first record said `0 ms` and it was a dead counter**: `clock()` measures nothing on HOS, so the one instrument placed to calibrate this number never wrote anything. Moved to `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`. Same family as S81's capped counters — a counter that is DISPLAYED is not a counter that is WRITTEN.
  **Value kept, and the reason matters more than the figure.** Half a second per unlock is affordable; the 600,000 rounds current guidance recommends would cost **5.5 s** here, so the console cannot buy modern resistance at any price it can pay. And above all: for a 4-digit PIN or a 3×3 pattern, the round count buys **almost nothing** against someone who has taken the card — ten thousand combinations fall in seconds on a GPU whatever the count. What protects those two methods is THROTTLING, which only exists as long as the card stays in the console. The rounds are therefore there for the PASSWORD, the only method with the entropy that makes them worthwhile.

- **2026-09-02 (RE-3)** — **THE BINARY SETTLES IT: `VideoFrame` byte 0 = codec in the HIGH nibble, version in the LOW one. AND RE-2 WAS WRONG.** `[C95+]` The official client's deserialiser (`@0xbfebb0`) switches on the HIGH nibble — 1 -> H.265, 2 -> AV1, 0 -> H.264 — and merely STORES the low nibble without ever comparing it; the enum thus set selects the NAL grammar at `@0xbfeb80` (`& 0x1F` against `>> 1 & 0x3F`). Verified with objdump, all three targets included.
  **Why RE-2 got it wrong, and the lesson is worth more than the result**: the census of the four values `0x02/0x12/0x22/0x32` was EXACT, but it was about the **outer STFP** header — ten bytes, TCP channels, whose low nibble must be 2 and whose HIGH nibble is a frame class (0 empty, 1 key frame, 2 ordinary, 3 ping). **Two headers of the same shape are stacked**, and we censused one while reasoning about the other. The corpus alone could not settle it: every capture is H.264, and `0x02` reads the same either way. It took the binary.
  **Real defect fixed**: `vid_reasm.c` read the nibbles the wrong way round; its guard let H.264 and H.265 through by coincidence and would have dropped **100 % of AV1 frames**. §3.46
  **Census**: 53,830 `VideoFrame` records, 26 capture sets, 4 port bases — byte 0 = `0x02` throughout.

- **2026-09-02 (RE-4)** — **THE KEY-FRAME EXTENSION CARRIES THE RESOLUTION AND THE FRAME RATE — L18'S OPEN QUESTION IS CLOSED.** `[C95+]` Byte 5 of `VideoFrame` is a COUNT of extension records, not a boolean. Each one is `[type<<4|ver][u16 LE length][payload]`, and only one type exists (`1/1`, length 10) — hence the 19 bytes we were skipping without understanding them. The payload reads `{u16 width, u16 height, f32 frame rate, 01, X}`: measured `2560x1440 @ 59.9198`, matching the register line of the same capture. **So the mode actually granted is in every key frame** — it never had to be asked of the server. §3.48

- **2026-09-02 (RE-1)** — **THREE QUESTIONS CLOSED WITHOUT A SINGLE NEW CAPTURE.** `[C95+]` All from data already in the repo since August, and two of them correct the repo's own documentation.
  1. **SUFP byte 10 is closed**: two values only, `0x00` and `0x01`, across **37,982 datagrams**. Bits 1 to 7 are never set. §3.45
  2. **A 1 Hz server beacon exists on the video channel** and we treat it as a chunk: 75 datagrams of 11 bytes, header only, subchannel 0, median interval 1.00 s. It is the "ping shape" the disassembly distinguishes on byte 0 — which `vid_wire.c` does not even copy. §3.45
  3. **The `:base+14` clipboard is censused in full**: 21 messages, seven shapes. **Type 3 exists and was documented nowhere**, and **type 4 carries the clipboard's CONTENT in both directions** — proven by `azertyazerty` (len 12) and by a 9-byte payload that is the scenario's `123456789`. `core/protocol/ctrl_comchan.h:20` describes it as `ACTIVE_APP_NAME`: **that is wrong**. §3.47
  **Method, and it applies going forward**: the SUFP header travels OUTSIDE the AEAD and the TLS channels are already in cleartext in the hook's `EVP_DECRYPT_OUT` records. TLS channels are NOT attributed by `fd` (it is -1) but by the `ssl=` pointer. And two traps produced silent zeros during this campaign: a `(?:[0-9a-f]{2} ){10}` pattern fails on the hexdump's **double space** at the 8th byte, and filtering by port without separating `UDP_RECVMSG` from `UDP_SENDMSG` mixes the server's stream with our own `gE`/`rG`.

- **2026-09-02 (RE-2)** — **`VideoFrame` BYTE 0: K15s REFUTES ITSELF BY ITS OWN CENSUS.** `[C95+]` The telemetry of `scenario_transport-tcp_20260829_000720` gives `codec = H264` and `62 - H264 - TCP` — a **single-codec** session. Yet byte 0 there takes four high nibbles (`0x02/0x12/0x22/0x32`). A codec does not change four times within a session: **the high nibble is not the codec**, and K15s's claim "high = codec, 0=H264 1=H265 2=AV1" is contradicted by the list K15s gives three lines above. Three readings now coexist in the repo (`core/protocol/vid_reasm.c:1226`, `§3.10 [C100]`, `K15s`) and **none is established**. A consistent but unproven reading: low = version (2 throughout), high = frame class. The instrument to settle it is already in the code (`[K15] VideoFrame header byte0`). §3.46
- **2026-08-29 (S109)** — **CRASH IN `nvtegra_transfer_data`, LOCATED BUT NOT YET EXPLAINED.** `[C90]` Atmosphère report `01788016921_010029b0118e8000.log`: *Data Abort*, faulting address `0x5586ba3000`, resolved by `addr2line` against the ELF of the build in use:

  ```
  PC        0x980c68   nvtegra_transfer_data (hwcontext_nvtegra.c)
  RA[00]    0x111e28   glue_decode_frame  (ctrl_session_glue.c:483)
  RA[01]    0x112ef0   dec_thread_fn      (ctrl_session_glue.c:632)
  ```

  The decode thread, inside the NVDEC -> CPU hardware transfer. **This is NOT the decoder teardown race** (`project_BUG2_decoder_race_fix`): the disassembly joins this thread before freeing the decoder, and the comment at `core/protocol/ctrl_session_glue.c:601` documents it.

  **Context of the incident**: an abnormal session where the control channel took 34 s to connect (see S108), the machine being in the middle of a reboot. The first frames arrived in that state.

  **What the existing protection does NOT cover**: `h264_decoder.c` disables acceleration after five CONSECUTIVE failures of `av_hwframe_transfer_data` — that is, after five `rc < 0`. A hardware fault INSIDE the driver never returns an error code: it takes the process down. The safeguard counts failures that will not happen.

  **Not reproduced**: the picture came back normally as soon as the machine finished rebooting, with the same binary. To reopen if a second report lands on the same stack — that is what would distinguish a defect in our path from a driver accident over an invalid surface. Refs: `core/media/h264_decoder.c:202`, `project_HW1_hwaccel_rc5_fix.md`.

- **2026-08-29 (L15) — CORRECTED THE SAME DAY, SEE BELOW.** `[C85]` The delivered frame rate does not follow the requested one, and **what bounds it is not yet known**.
  **Measurements, same VM, same handheld Wi-Fi link, animated content**:
  | requested | received | bitrate |
  |---|---|---|
  | 120 fps | **43.0** (max 44.4) | 5.7 Mbps |
  | 120 fps | **68.9** (max 73.6) | — |
  | 60 fps | **60.0** | 8.2 Mbps |
  Two sessions at 120 fps requested return 43 and 69: **the difference comes from the content or the link, not from the setting**. This is the repo's documented trap — three samples minimum, a single session's variance is enormous (`project_iter_2026-05-06_bandwidth_caps`) — and it was walked into again here by concluding from ONE pair.
  **HYPOTHESIS REFUTED the same day**: "the VM's screen mode (`2560x1440 @ 59.94`, first in the §3.8b list) caps the frame rate at 60". One session returned **68.9 fps mean and 73.6 peak** with that very mode announced. The first entry of the list is therefore NOT a delivery ceiling — and `ctrl_session.c`'s "1st = current accepted" annotation must not be read as one. Requesting 120 fps does have an effect: it goes past 60.
  **What remains true and useful**: `kNotifyResolution` (f14) only writes `Resolution { f1=width, f2=height }`, whereas the mode-list entries decode as `{ f1=w, f2=h, f3=fps fixed32 }` — a third field never written. The message is in any case off by default (`SHADOW_SEND_NRES=0`) because the official client does not emit it on resize (line 707). It remains an RE lead, but it NO LONGER explains the observation.
  **Added**: `[L15]` compares the requested frame rate against the received one every 5 s, over the last slice rather than since the start, as soon as the gap exceeds 15 %. Both numbers already existed — `[Q1]` at the start of the session, a cumulative counter afterwards — and nobody had put them side by side. Refs: `core/protocol/ctrl_session.c` (`[L15]`), `core/protocol/ctrl_msgs.c:400`. Cross-ref: §3.8b, line 707.

- **2026-08-29 (L5)** — **THE SESSION MEASURES ITSELF: PER-STAGE LATENCY, WITH PERCENTILES.** `[C95+]` No instrument covered the most expensive link in the video path: `[L1]` runs from "frame assembled" to "frame decoded", `stat_latency` runs from "frame pushed" to "frame drawn", and the hold in `g_display_buf` falls **exactly between the two**. Worse, `stat_latency` is only fed under `if (show_perf_stats && hud_.visible())` — a counter that is only WRITTEN while you look at it, and that leaves no trace in the log.
  **Added**: `demo/src/streaming/latence.{c,h}` + `tests/test_latency.c` (22nd suite, 135,582 checks). Nine stages reported every 10 s as `n / mean / p50 / p90 / p99 / window worst / session worst`: `video/rafale` (first to last chunk), `video/file-dec`, `video/decode`, `video/file-aff`, `video/televerse`, `video/cadence`, `video/bout`, `entree/envoi` (device read -> `wolfSSL_write`), `audio/file` (real depth of the output queue).
  **End to end with no common clock**: the `VideoFrame` timestamp is the SERVER's, so the absolute difference means nothing. We publish the BASE (the smallest difference observed = the fastest trip), the distribution of `difference - base` (the ADDED latency), and the DRIFT of that base from one window to the next. The carrier is `pts`, used as a **KEY** and never as a value: it is the only field that crosses libavcodec, and its value goes through `pts_ms = ts / 90`, a division whose unit is doubtful.
  **THE TIMESTAMP'S UNIT IS MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED**: every report publishes `rapport = server units / local µs`. ~1.000 confirms microseconds, ~0.090 would say 90 kHz, ~0.001 would say milliseconds. A single session settles the question that has been open since 2026-08-29.
  **Dead counter fixed along the way**: `buf_drop_count` (the audout path, the only one used on console) was absent from `audio_stats_t`. The panel, the `hud/audio_ring` line and the FLAC report therefore showed **always 0 on Switch** — a saturated output queue was invisible by construction. `audio_decoder_get_stats` now adds it to `buffers_dropped`.
  **Measured cost** (i7-9750H, 20 M calls): 15.2 ns per `clock_gettime`, 3.7 ns per deposit, i.e. **0.13 µs per frame** for seven clocks; Switch ESTIMATE ×8 to ×10 = ~1.3 µs for a 20 ms frame. No allocation, no lock in the hot path. `SHADOW_LATENCE=0` restores the uninstrumented app; `SHADOW_LATENCE_MS` sets the period (10,000 by default).
  **Two defects in the instrument found BY RUNNING IT, not by re-reading it** (and pinned by the test): a base frozen on the first sample lost 499 frames out of 500 when that first frame was a spike; and the drift computed after the write shifted the line by a whole window. Both produced perfectly plausible figures.
- **2026-08-29 (K15s)** — **THE KEY FRAME ARRIVES. WE ARE THE ONES THROWING IT AWAY.** `[C95+]` **Root cause of K15r, and it refutes the "remaining lead" noted just below**: the server sends SPS+PPS+IDR **spontaneously, 74 ms after the register packet**, without anything having to be asked. So no request encoding could have fixed anything — the server had already answered.
  **The defect is one line**: `core/protocol/ctrl_video_tcp.c:160` routes every frame of class `0x12` to `curseur_cb`. On the VIDEO instance (`ctx->vtcp`) that callback is **NULL** — `ctrl_video_tcp_set_cursor_cb` is only called on `ctx->vst`, the cursor channel. So the **only** frame of the session that carries the key frame is silently destroyed, after having incremented `frames_received`. Hence K15r's exact picture: normal bitrate, `decoded` tracking `video`, `sps=0 pps=0 idr_t=0`, black screen, zero errors.
  **Source**: framing simulated over the ENTIRE TCP video stream of `captures/scenario_transport-tcp_20260829_000720` — 12,330 reads, 174,739,413 bytes, **2698 frames, 0 misalignments**. Classes on `:base+10`: `0x02` ×14 (empty payload), **`0x12` ×1 (the key frame)**, `0x22` ×2586 (ordinary frame, NAL type 1 in 2586/2586), `0x32` ×97 (PING, ~1/s).
  **Two established facts to correct along the way**:
    - **K15 read the nibbles the wrong way round** (KB §3.41), and `vid_reasm.c` with it: in the `VideoFrame` header, the **LOW nibble is the version** (2) and the **HIGH one the codec** (0=H264, 1=H265, 2=AV1). It works by coincidence on H264 (`0x02`) and H265 (`0x12`), and would drop **100 % of AV1 frames**.
    - **K15q is built on a mis-attributed socket**: the five 10-byte writes `00 00 00 NN 00…` taken for a key-frame request are on `:base+14`, the **clipboard**. So we are writing a clipboard message into the video opcode stream. To be removed.
  **The real video uplink** (exhaustive survey, 1608 writes, four shapes and nothing else): `41 01 00 14 00`+hash20 ×1; `gE` 15 B ×1413 (13.3/s); `70` ×97 (**1.09 s**, not 7); `50 49 01` ×97. **Zero `0x64`, zero `iP`, zero `rG`.**
  Full porting plan, in deliverable stages each with its criterion written beforehand: `docs/PLAN_video_TCP_port.md`.
- **2026-08-29 (K15r)** — **THE TCP PORT WORKS, EXCEPT FOR THE KEY FRAME — AND THAT IS THE ONLY THING MISSING.** `[C95+]` An honest state of play after eight desktop trials.
  **What works**: the `:base+10` channel opens in STFP+TLS, frames arrive (43 fps, 4.8 Mbit/s), the framing reads without a single resynchronisation error, and the slice ratio is **50.0 %** — the reference value of a healthy stream.
  **What blocks**: `idr_t = 0` in every session. Dumping the first bytes shows that **EVERY frame is a non-IDR slice** (`02 [ts] 00 | 00 00 00 01 | 61`, type 1). So the decoder receives slices without ever receiving SPS, PPS or IDR: "decoder stalled, 1725 frames supplied with no output", and **nothing complains anywhere but in that counter**.
  **THREE KEY-FRAME REQUESTS TRIED, THREE FAILURES** — recorded so they are not tried again:
    1. `0x64` on the STFP channel (S54's refresh) — sent, no effect;
    2. `Request_Flush` on the control channel (N40, case 7) — 5 sent, no effect;
    3. a bare 10-byte STFP header, class 0, counter at byte 3 — a shape observed in what the official client writes (5 occurrences), analogous to the UDP `iP` which also carries a counter — sent (rc=10), no effect.
  **Two structural defects found and fixed along the way**, both invisible: (K15p) **the whole return loop was guarded by `udp_video >= 0`** and therefore DEAD in TCP mode — nothing could ask for anything; (K15l) the TCP path fed **no volume counter at all**, `avg_kbps` returned 0.0 over 1768 frames — the defect family V12b had just purged, back through the porting door.
  **Remaining lead, not tried**: the official capture was taken on a session ALREADY in progress; the key frame may be sent spontaneously at channel registration, and what we are missing would be in the order or the content of our announcement, not in a request. To be checked by capturing the START of an official session in the `reliability` profile.
- **2026-08-29 (K15m)** — **THE VIDEO ARRIVES OVER TCP. THE PORT WORKS.** `[C95+]` First attempt, desktop client, `SHADOW_VIDEO_NET_TCP=1`:

  ```
  [1.787] [K15i] video demandee en TCP : pas de socket UDP sur :12010
  [2.051] [K15k] canal video TCP ouvert sur :12010 (STFP + TLS)
  [2.133] [K15k] trame video TCP #1 : 678 octets
  ...
  1644 images | 43,3 im/s | 4,8 Mbit/s | NAL top=1645 bot=1645 (50,0 %)
  ```

  **The bottom-slice ratio is exactly 50.0 %**, the repo's reference value for a healthy stream — it is the metric by which G4's picture bug showed up (it fell far below). `decrypt=0/0` is correct: there is no application-level encryption on this path, TLS handles it. `disp=0` means nothing here, the headless binary having a stubbed display — **never conclude anything visual from `shadow-test-cli`**.
  **What made the port short**: an STFP frame's payload carries the same `VideoFrame` header as UDP (K15d), so `emit_legacy` is shared as is — de-duplication, header stripping, access-unit aggregation (G40), the whole emission path with its four campaigns of fixes. The port reduces to: open the TCP+TLS socket on `:base+10`, do NOT create the UDP socket, and pass the payload through.
- **2026-08-29 (K15l)** — **THE TCP PATH FED NO VOLUME COUNTER AT ALL.** `[C95+]` First attempt: `avg_kbps=0.0` while 1768 frames were arriving. Only UDP bytes were counted. **This is exactly the defect family V12b had just purged** — a displayed counter that is not written — and it would have come back through the porting door. Fixed before anyone relied on it: the second attempt returns 4.8 Mbit/s.
- **2026-08-29 (K15d)** — **THE TCP PORT'S LOCK GIVES WAY: THE VIDEO PAYLOAD IS IDENTICAL TO UDP'S.** `[C95+]` Analysis of the **repo's first capture in the `reliability` profile** (`captures/scenario_transport-tcp_20260829_000720`, 3.36 GB, made 2026-08-29). **The STFP framing is proven empirically**: across 14,043 SSL reads of more than 100 bytes, **1470 satisfy `announced length + 10 == block size` EXACTLY** — the probability of an arbitrary u32 landing right is of the order of 1 in 4 billion, so the 10-byte header and the length at bytes 2..5 are established on the wire and no longer only in the disassembly. And byte 0 of those 1470 frames takes only **two values, `0x12` and `0x22`**, the ones K15c predicted. **WHAT THE PAYLOAD CONTAINS**:

  ```
  0x22 (1372 frames, the video) : 02 ff 24 d8 3c 00 | 00 00 01 61 ...
                                  ^^ ^^^^^^^^^^^ ^^   ^^^^^^^^ Annex-B
                                  |  timestamp   flags
                                  VideoFrame header, 6 bytes

  0x12 (98 frames, 4142 B)      : 04 00 01 00 ... = the CURSOR
                                  (4142 = 10 + 36 + 4096 pixels 32x32 BGRA)
  ```

  **The TCP video payload carries EXACTLY the same `VideoFrame` header as UDP** — `[0x02][u32 timestamp][flags]`, six bytes, then the Annex-B start code. **The STFP header does not REPLACE the VideoFrame header: it stacks on top of it.** The "variable-size shape-2 sub-header" that was blocking the port does not exist on this path. **Direct consequence: `emit_legacy` (vid_reasm.c) needs NO modification at all** — the trap K15 flagged ("passing it a TCP payload as is would make it skip the first NAL") is refuted, the layout being the same on both sides. What remains to write reduces to: open the TCP+TLS socket instead of the UDP one, read frames by their announced length, and strip the ten header bytes before passing the payload to the existing emission path.
- **2026-08-29 (K15e)** — **Q3 SETTLED: THE TCP VIDEO CHANNEL IS WRAPPED IN TLS.** `[C95+]` The stream of `fd=346` (`:11010`, `type=TCP`) begins with `16 03 03 00 7a 02 00 00 76 03 03 ...` — a ServerHello. So it is **`VideoSslTcpChannel`**, and the cleartext lives in the `SSL_READ` / `EVP_DECRYPT_OUT` blocks. `tls_chan.c` already provides that path (no SNI, no ALPN, no validation). **The profile also switches INPUT to TCP** (`:11012 type=TCP`, where it was UDP+DTLS) and **leaves the gamepad on UDP** (`:11013 type=UDP`) — exactly the map K15 had deduced from the binary. The port does not change. Measured bitrate: 167.8 MB of video cleartext in ~60 s, i.e. ~22 Mbit/s.
- **2026-08-29 (D4d)** — **98 OUTAGES OUT OF 100 HAPPEN AT FULL SIGNAL.** `[C80]` A free reading, signal strength having been logged at every outage since D4: across the two instrumented console sessions, **67 + 31 = 98 outages at `force=3`** (the maximum) and **only 2 at `force=2`**. This WEAKENS the "plain radio degradation" hypothesis without killing it: `nifmGetInternetConnectionStatus`'s indicator is a coarse 0-to-3 scale, sampled at the INSTANT of the trigger, so a brief RF dropout could escape it. To be set against the Ethernet control when that becomes possible — the user has neither a USB-Ethernet adapter nor a free dock for now.
- **2026-08-29 (V12b)** — **THE FIVE DEAD COUNTERS ARE REMOVED, NOT WIRED UP.** `[C95+]` `chunks_late`, `chunks_backward`, `seq_window_drop`, `chunks_tail_recovered`, `reasm_size_change` disappear from `ctrl_session_stats`, from the bridge, from the stats line and from the measurement JSON. **Three of them have NO referent left in the code** (the notions went away with the old reassembly machinery, commit `19229d0`) and **the other two only lived in the log line**. Wiring them up would have meant inventing an increment point for a notion the code no longer has, which would produce a meaningless number — the same defect, worse. **If a concept comes back, its counter comes back with it.** Practical consequence: `queues=` and `skip=` disappear from the stats line, where they had shown zero since 25/08 and where they made me write, on 28/08, that "nothing arrives after the flush" — a sentence that was worth nothing. **A counter that is displayed is not a counter that is written.**
- **2026-08-28 (G57)** — **FORCE FEEDBACK WORKS, FROM WINDOWS TO THE JOY-CONS.** `[C95+]` RESOLVED. The VM had been sending us force feedback all along and we were discarding it. Format established from OUR console logs (the official captures are all zeros, for lack of a game that rumbles): `04 [id] 07 [low] [high]`, no duration, 33 msg/s. **The method that unblocked it**: four test actions in the settings, which bypass the protocol and touch the hardware alone — without them, "it does not rumble" and "nothing asked it to rumble" stayed indistinguishable, and that is exactly what had happened (a session's 6 kVibration messages all carried `0,0`). **Three silent traps avoided**: the session thread would write into Borealis' `PadState`; `sendRumble()` derives the frequency from the amplitude and gives 10 Hz at 20 % power; a `StyleSet` passed where a `StyleTag` is expected compiles without a word. **A methodological failure worth recording**: I shipped the first version WITHOUT A SINGLE WITNESS, in a repo whose central lesson is that nothing complains — the investigation had listed this link and I skipped it. Adjustable intensity from 0 to 100 % (G57d). See §3.44.
- **2026-08-28 (V11b, WITHDRAWN — WRONG CONCLUSION)** — I had written "first gaming session with V11: zero truncated frames in 40 minutes". **V11 WAS NOT IN THAT BINARY.** The user pointed it out; the chronology confirms it (V11 committed at 21:08 and K15c at 21:19, but only pushed around 23:2x, while the session had been running since ~22:50). The binary that was running carried AUD15, AUD16, D4/D4b, G55 and SEC1 — not V11, not K15c. **What the session actually measures**: `tronq=0` over 3,146,354 chunks and 0.11 % loss **WITHOUT** V11, and 3.0 % of time with no video against 32 % in the 5 pm session. Both figures remain facts; they simply say nothing about V11. **Useful corollary**: the truncation V11 fixes is SCENE-dependent — the investigation had put it between 0.06 % and 2.6 % of frames depending on the content — and that session produced none. **V11's effect remains entirely to be measured.** Lesson: I attributed to a fix a result obtained without it, for want of checking which binary was running. The log did say so — all it took was looking for a V11 witness, which does not exist, and that is precisely why a silent fix ought to carry one.
- **2026-08-28 (D4c)** — **THE §3.34 OUTAGE GOES FROM n=1 TO n=13, AND THE DISCRIMINANT IS UNANIMOUS.** `[C95+]` Of the gaming session's 31 outages, **13 exceed 1.5 s — and all 13 received control frames DURING them**. So the host was talking without interruption in 13 cases out of 13 while the media path was dead. K15's objection ("if the server cut the subscription, it would also cut the TCP") was already refuted at n=1; it now is unambiguously. Outages under 1.5 s are correctly labelled "too short to conclude" by D4b — the control channel's cadence being ~500 ms, counting zero frames there proves nothing. **The STFP port becomes the project's highest-impact action.**
- **2026-08-28 (AUD15b)** — **PING DRIFT IS REFUTED, AND WITH IT AUD14'S LEADING CANDIDATE.** `[C95+]` AUD14 named our 7.5 s ping on `:base+30` as the probable cause of `CHANNEL_DOWN(AUDIO)`, on an arithmetic argument: 294.3 s of session = 39.24 periods, and **45 ms of mean drift per period was needed** for ping #39 to land in the 40-130 ms window that precedes the notification. Measured across 34 sampled pings: interval **7500 to 7520 ms, mean 7504.3**, i.e. **4.3 ms of drift per period and 167 ms cumulative over 39 periods** — an order of magnitude below what the hypothesis required. **The ping cannot have landed in that window through drift.** The cause of the audio channel's teardown becomes unknown again. So the AUD15 log served to kill a hypothesis in one session, which is its job.

It was in **Opus**, which matches the user's observation ("in normal OPUS mode I had no sound trouble") and AUD13. So the AUD16 safety net never had a chance to fire: it remains an executable hypothesis, not a validated one. Testing it requires a **FLAC** session.
- **2026-08-28 (INV1)** — **67 CAPTURE DIRECTORIES, 30 IN THE CATALOGUE.** `[C95+]` Full inventory, measured against the files rather than taken from a report: **37 directories missing from `CAPTURES_KB.md`, including the repo's three largest deposits**. `tools/query_captures.sh` — the tool meant to avoid a pointless capture — only listed `*.pcap` and **therefore saw NONE** of the 6.9 GB of hook logs. Fixed. Corrected, the claims struck through rather than erased. **Corpus trap worth remembering**: in five captures (`manual_*`, `scripted_*` of 21/08) the hook logs NO streaming-socket `CONNECT` at all — `+14`, `+15` and `+32` are undetectable there **by construction**, and a negative drawn from those files is worth nothing. **None of the six open questions was closed** by this material: the refutation pass killed every candidate answer. That is a result, not a failure — it saves six pointless searches.
- **2026-08-28 (SEC2)** — **A KEYLOGGER HAD BEEN RUNNING FOR 24 HOURS.** `[C95+]` `tools/watch_keyboard.py`, started on 27/08 for the K7 campaign (key identification), was never stopped: **28,315 lines**, from 27/08 21:23 to 28/08 21:29, while **only 52 lines** belong to the Shadow capture window. It logs EVERY keystroke on the machine, not just those aimed at the VM. Process stopped. The file is not touched without the user's decision: it is theirs, and it is for them to say what becomes of it. **Same family as SEC1, and the same lesson: exploratory instrumentation must be removed once its object is understood.** SEC1 was a dump forgotten in the code, SEC2 a process forgotten on the machine.
- **2026-08-28 (K15c)** — **THE STFP LENGTH IS A u32, AND NO CAPTURE COULD HAVE SAID SO.** `[C95+]` K15's Q5 is settled — **by the disassembly, not by the wire**, and that is the investigation's most instructive result. The length field is a **little-endian u32 at bytes 2..5**: `ShadowPCDisplay` @0xd61030 does `movzbl (%rsi),%eax ; and $0xf,%eax ; cmp $0x2,%al ; jne reject ; mov 0x2(%rsi),%eax` — and `8b 46 02` is a `mov r32, m32`; a u16 would have encoded as `0f b7 46 02` (`movzwl`). Verified on two independent binaries, five classes carrying the same vtable quadruple. **STFP header = 10 bytes**: `[b0 low nibble = 2, mandatory][b1][u32 LE length][u32 LE timestamp]`. **THE TRAP, AND THE LESSON**: across **~3900 STFP frames** drawn from **6.9 GB** of captures — the repo's 30 logs plus `/tmp/shadow_plaintext.log`, inventoried for the first time — bytes 4-5 are zero **without a single exception**, and there are only **four frame sizes**: 10, 46, 302, 4142. The largest is structural (10 + 36 + 4096 pixels 32x32 BGRA), i.e. **6.3 % of a u16's ceiling**. **So u16 and u32 return the SAME value on 100 % of the bytes we possess**: the corpus could not settle it, and its silence read as proof — our comment said word for word "p[4] and p[5] are always zero across the 140 frames analysed". **"Those bytes are always zero in the captures" NEVER tells you a field's width: it tells you the range the captured scenario exercised.** Same family as G4. Practical consequence: nothing changes today (the cursor caps at 4142 bytes) but a 1080p key frame on a TCP video channel is 200 to 400 KB — our u16 read would have truncated the length and **desynchronised the parser forever, without a single error**. Fixed, with its counter-case in `tests/test_cursor_wire.c` (196,608 bytes, and 65,536 which is the first value where the two readings diverge). **Scope**: STFP covers `VideoTcpChannel`, `CursorTcpChannel`, `AudioOutTcpChannel` and their SSL variants — **not** the `:base+14` clipboard (big-endian, different framing, to be settled before implementing it), **not** the `:base+15` SFTP, **not** the `:base+11` control channel. **The rule "this protocol's TCP channels carry a 32-bit length" is FALSE**: three of them carry it on 16 bits, including STFP's own UPLINK direction. **Still open**: the shape-2 video sub-header is VARIABLE in size, computed by `0xbfebb0`, unread — that is what blocks the port, not Q5.
- **2026-08-28 (V11)** — **WE WERE DISCARDING FRAME TAILS, AND IT WAS CERTAIN, NOT PROBABILISTIC.** `[C95+]` `vid_wire_parse_hdr` demanded `n > 11 + 28` of EVERY packet, assuming each one carries a nonce and a tag. **That is false: only an AEAD chunk (`b10 & 1`) carries them.** A CONTINUATION chunk is cleartext H.264 NAL, with no overhead: its legitimate minimum is ONE useful byte, so `n > 11`. The comment justifying the old bound asserted that "the wire has never shown a continuation that short" — **the wire shows them constantly**: across four cleartext captures of the official client, **98 packets of 12 to 39 bytes** arrive on `:base+10`, and **all 98 carry `chunk_idx == max_field`** (the LAST chunk) with `b10 == 0`, without exception. And the server re-sends that tail ~3 ms later identically: **BOTH copies were being dropped**, so the loss was certain. This is exactly the G4 family — the last chunk carries the tail of the bottom slice — by another route. Measured effect: **~1.1 key frames requested per second** (`idr_t=46` in 41 s) on a session with short tails, against **0.06/s** without (`perdus=0`, `idr_t=2` in 35 s): this is the G5/G37 autofocus symptom, from a new cause. Tail length is a division remainder, so it depends on the CONTENT and not on the network: from 0.06 % to 2.6 % of frames depending on the scene. Toggle `SHADOW_SHORT_TAIL=0`, placed at the caller (the module stays pure) and restoring the old rejection **exactly where it used to happen**. **Corrects V9**: the "short packets" V9 counted were not all beacons — the 39-byte ones were legitimate frame tails that we were destroying. V9's clock defect remains real for bare headers (`len=11`).
- **2026-08-28 (V12)** — **SEVEN LOSS COUNTERS WERE INCREMENTED NOWHERE.** `[C95+]` `chunks_orphan_dup`, `chunks_orphan_lost`, `chunks_late`, `chunks_backward`, `seq_window_drop`, `chunks_tail_recovered`, `reasm_size_change`: **zero increments in the whole tree**, verified at HEAD. Yet they were declared, copied into the GUI bridge, printed in the stats line AND in the measurement JSON. Commit `19229d0` (2026-08-25) had removed their increments along with the old reassembly machinery, leaving the fields behind. **Their zero therefore read as a measurement when it was dead code** — and it did mislead: my V10 analysis this morning cited "`chunks_late` and `chunks_tail_recovered` at zero" as proof that repairs were arriving in time. That sentence was worth nothing. `queues=0` and `skip=0` in every log in the repo mean nothing either. **This is the exact counterpart of `feedback_verify_grep_measurements.md`'s lesson, one level down: a counter that is displayed is not a counter that is written.**
- **2026-08-28 (V13)** — **THE 4 % OF ORPHANS ARE BENIGN, AND DEMONSTRATED.** `[C95+]` **The server sends the last chunk of every frame TWICE**, ~3 ms apart. The second copy arrives after the flush, finds no open buffer, and counts as an orphan. Replay of the state machine over 5000 real chunks (`raw_chunks.bin`): 76 complete flushes, **76 orphans, all carrying `chunk_idx == count-1`, all with payloads IDENTICAL TO THE BIT** to the copy already filed. The send-timestamp difference (bytes 6-9, median 3313 µs) distinguishes network duplication from a server re-send: duplication would copy the bytes identically. **It is the server protecting itself — from what G4 cost us a whole campaign.** The "4 %" is not a physical quantity but `1/(chunks per frame + 1)`: **ONE orphan per multi-chunk frame**, a ceiling never crossed across 47 sessions (max 0.9725). Correlation with chunks/frame: 0.817. **REFUTED along the way**: the accused branch (a different `max_chunks`) never fires (0 out of 5000 on our side, 0.3 % of orphans across 160,000 official chunks); no correlation with quality; and the "benign" reading I was proposing was also wrong in its mechanism — it is not the first chunk of a new frame, it is the last chunk of the old one.
- **2026-08-28 (SEC1)** — **OUR OWN LOG DUMPED A PRIVATE KEY, EVERY SESSION.** `[C95+]` Found by the audio investigation, which refused to write a document asking for other dumps to be turned on without flagging this one. `ctrl_session.c` dumped the first 512 bytes of the reply to the batch of eight announcements **unconditionally**, **in hex THEN in ascii** — and that 522-byte body carries the SFTP channel `:base+15`'s **OpenSSH ed25519 private key in cleartext**. **57 logs on this disk contained it, twice each.** A log gets passed on, archived, pasted into a bug report. Verified: **no git-tracked file** carried it (`captures/` is ignored) — the leak was local, and the 57 files were redacted in place (124 lines) rather than destroyed, to keep the measurement material. Fix: the dump is bounded to 24 bytes (the field tag, the sessionId, the port — everything still read by eye) and **any body >= 400 bytes is refused**, a size below which the key cannot fit. `SHADOW_DUMP_M13=1` restores the full dump, knowingly. The dump existed to explore the reply (H1.V8, May 2026); its content has since been entirely decoded — supported modes (FPS1), transport and port granted per channel (K15b) — it taught nothing more. **Lesson: exploratory instrumentation must be removed once its object is understood, otherwise it outlives its reason for being and becomes a liability.**
- **2026-08-28 (AUD14)** — **AUDIO: THE "AND IT NEVER COMES BACK" HALF IS ENTIRELY ON OUR SIDE.** `[C95+]` 18-agent investigation; **3 leads out of 4 refuted**, one survives. **REFUTED — the collapse at t=35 s is not a defect**: those figures came from the log's THIRD session, in **Opus**, and the counter does not collapse, it drops back to the SILENCE regime and then climbs again on its own (6006 -> 6478 -> 6528 -> 6578 -> **7222**). Three concordant proofs: AUD2's size buckets show `47B` growing while `100-260B` stays frozen (47 B on the wire = 8 B of cleartext = minimal frame); the decoder announces "480 samples for 18 B"; **and the official client does exactly the same** — 10 to 11 packets/s when the VM is silent, 159 to 643 when it plays. So my AUD13 entry needs correcting: there were not two defects, there is one plus a non-event. **REFUTED — SUFP saturation is a thermometer of sound that WORKS** **[Instrument turned off by default on 2026-09-11 (ING-A3): that SUFP fallback completed no `:base+30` frame at all, and `sufp: window full` no longer appears without `SHADOW_AUD_SUFP=1`.]**, not a distress signal: it counts frames too large for one datagram, rises when the VM plays loudly and stops when the channel dies. Reading it as a failure signal reverses the arrow. **NOT ESTABLISHED — the cause of the `CHANNEL_DOWN(AUDIO)`**: the only documented mechanism that produces one is OUR OWN emission on `:base+30` (KB §3.38, 43 sessions out of 69), and after start-up all that is left is the `0x70` ping every 7.5 s. The arithmetic is compatible (294.3 s = 39.24 periods) **but the cadence DRIFTS** — `t_last = now_ms` instead of `+= 7500` — and that drift has never been measured because **the ping is not logged**. Stays at C60. **ESTABLISHED at C95+ and entirely our share**: `g_audio_channel_down` is written by `ctrl_tcp.c` and **has NO reader**; AUD5, the channel's only recovery, is guarded by `udp_cursor_pkts == 0`, a condition false from the first packet — hence disarmed for life; and **there is no stall detector on `:base+30` at all**, where the video has `d2_last_rx_ms`, `[D2]` and S41. Without the server's `[S56]`, this death would have left **no trace** — and §3.34 shows the server does not always announce it. Fix to write: make the handling symmetric with the video's.
- **2026-08-28 (D4)** — **THE §3.34 OUTAGE MEASURED: 32 % OF A SESSION WITH NO VIDEO, AND THE HOST TALKS THROUGHOUT.** `[C95+]` First instrumented session (`captures/tests_autonomes_20260828/D4_wifi_MESURE.log`, 844 s, handheld Wi-Fi, signal 3/3):
  - **68 outages**, totalling **268 s with no video** — i.e. **32 % of the session**. Distribution: 26 under 500 ms, 10 under 1 s, 15 under 2 s, **17 beyond 2 s**.
  - **The worst lasts 143.6 s** (t=597 -> t=740.6). Verified on the counters and not on the alert alone: `video=444271` is FROZEN from t=599.5 to t=740, zero datagrams.
  - **305 control frames RECEIVED during those 143 s.** So the host was talking to us without interruption while the media path was dead.
  - **S41 tries and fails**: it resends the video registration every 2 s (~140 attempts in the single long outage) without ever restoring the stream.
- **WHAT THIS CHANGES FOR THE TCP TRANSPORT (K15).** The K15 report's main objection was: "if the server cuts the subscription, it would also cut a TCP stream". **It is REFUTED**: the server cuts nothing — it keeps answering on `:base+11` throughout the failure, it is the UDP path alone that dies. K15's opportunity verdict also rested on "n=1, cause unknown" and on UDP loss of 0.008 to 0.099 %; those two measurements remain true but they are about PACKET LOSS, not about these clean outages, which are of a different nature and a wholly different cost. **To be re-evaluated.**
- **2026-08-28 (D4b)** — **TWO DEFECTS IN MY OWN INSTRUMENT, ONE OF WHICH ASSERTED SOMETHING FALSE.** `[C95+]` (1) The duration was computed AFTER the reference timestamp was updated: all 68 outages were logged as `0 ms`, the histogram entirely in the first bucket. The real durations had to be recomputed by hand from the log's timestamps. (2) The verdict "the host went quiet too: machine or link" was asserted as soon as zero control frames arrived during the outage — but the control channel's cadence is about 500 ms, so on a 300 ms outage that zero says NOTHING. We now only conclude beyond 1.5 s, and below that threshold we write it. An instrument that concludes wrongly is worse than no instrument.
- **2026-08-28 (K15b)** — **THE SERVER GRANTS TCP FOR VIDEO, AND ON THE SAME PORT.** `[C95+]` Two of K15's headline questions closed without a guided capture, simply by sending the request from our own client (`SHADOW_VIDEO_NET_TCP=1`, added along with the f4 field fix). Comparison of the server's grants for the video channel:

  ```
  UDP (default)   22 07 10 02       20 01 28 e2 36   -> f3 ABSENT     f5 = 7010
  TCP (requested) 22 09 10 02 18 01 20 01 28 e2 36   -> f3 = 1 (TCP)  f5 = 7010
  ```

  - **Q1 closed**: the server GRANTS, it does not downgrade. It sends `18 01` back in its grant. The string `video was not granted but got fallback offer` does not appear.
  - **Q2 closed**: the port does not move — `f5 = 7010 = base+10` in both cases. K15 deduced it at `[C80]` from the port formula; it is now measured.
  - The bootstrap runs to completion (`8 grants out of 8`, `bootstrap=1`) and zero frames arrive, which is expected: we do not know how to receive STFP. **So the port is not blocked by the server**; it is blocked only by the ~500 lines of the receive path (K15 §5.4/§5.5).
  - Still open: Q3 (bare TCP or TLS), Q4 (framing), Q5 (width of the STFP length field), Q6 (uplink). All of them require READING the bytes of a TCP stream, so either the guided capture or a first implementation.
  - **K15's opportunity verdict is unchanged and unfavourable**: UDP loss measured on console is 0.008 to 0.099 %, TCP forbids the latency policy that discards the oldest frame, and it cuts adaptive bitrate. Knowing that it is POSSIBLE does not say it is desirable.
  - Trial note: the control session failed before bootstrap (`[sse] reconnect #10 ... http_status=404`) then **segfaulted on exit**. An exit path after a failed bootstrap — to look at, not investigated.
- **2026-08-28 (S77)** — **TWO CURSORS: THE SECOND ONE WAS NOT OURS.** `[C95+]` Reported in session. The code already forbade drawing the synthetic arrow AND the received sprite (`sprite_dessine` guards the fallback), so the duplicate could not come from our two renderers. Console trial with BOTH turned off (`SHADOW_CURSOR_SPRITE=0 SHADOW_CURSOR_ARROW=0`): **exactly one pointer remains, in the right place.** **So Windows composites its cursor into the video stream.** What we drew on top was a duplicate — and it separated as soon as our estimated position diverged from the real one, which happens as soon as a game recentres the mouse (cf. `SHADOW_INPUT_ABS`). A reminder that takes on its full meaning here: **the server only sends the pointer's SHAPE, never its position** (S54, measured across 55 frames all at zero) — so we were drawing it at the position WE were tracking. Defaults moved to 0 on both sides; `=1` restores each renderer. Consequence to note: the `:base+20` channel is now useless in our client, and `SHADOW_VIDEO_TCP` (which opens it) goes into an 8 s timeout ×4 intermittently (K15) — a candidate for being off by default, to be measured.
- **2026-08-28 (AUD13, PARTIALLY REFUTED by AUD14)** — **losing the sound would be tied to FLAC.** `[C60]` User observation: "in normal OPUS mode I had no sound trouble". Verified in the log of the session that lost the sound: it was in **FLAC** (`[32.253] [K14] codec audio demande : FLAC`, `[33.177] audio: FLAC detecte sur :base+30`). And the reassembly window's evictions start **ten seconds after the switch to FLAC** (`[43.142] sufp: window full evict_count=100`) then climb without interruption to `2000` at t=63 s, before the `CHANNEL_DOWN canal=AUDIO` at t=291 s. Plausible mechanism, **not established**: FLAC frames are much larger than Opus ones, hence multi-chunk, hence they go through the reassembler that Opus bypasses. **Immediate, measured workaround: stay on "Normal" (Opus).** The investigation under way must test its conclusion against this discriminant.
- **2026-08-28 (G56)** — **BYTE 3 OF THE kPlug IS THE GAMEPAD TYPE, AND THE ENUM IS CLOSED.** `[C95+]` Lead given by the official launcher's "Xbox Input" mode. Sweep of 0 to 9 on desktop then confirmation on console: with value 0, **Steam shows "Xbox 360 Controller"**. The wire enum is **the reverse of the pool order** (`Xbox360=0, XboxOne=1, Dualshock4=2, ControllerLeft=3, ControllerRight=4, Default=5`), and the pool was NOT sorted alphabetically, unlike the ones that trapped K13 and K14 — so the lesson is "check the pool's order", not "never trust it". **The server refuses above 5**: kReply with an error flag at byte 1, a code at byte 7 (`OnGamepadReplyError`), and **no kVibration** — the rumble burst only follows an accepted announcement, it is the most reliable witness of a device being created and it reads without looking at Windows. **We were announcing `02` = DualShock 4**, an artefact of the 26/08 capture made with a DS4: precisely the case Shadow's "Improved compatibility" setting fixes. Default moved to 0 (XInput), adjustable in the UI. See §3.43.
- **2026-08-28 (G55c)** — **GAMEPAD WORKING END TO END ON CONSOLE.** `[C95+]` RESOLVED. Trial on the Switch with the G54+G55 binary: `socket seul (pas de registre en clair)` — the key migration did neutralise the SD card's `settings.txt`; three inputs discarded by the order guard; `[G55] PREMIER octet du canal manette : annonce de branchement`; **zero `CHANNEL_DOWN`** (against one per session before); and 3 `kReply` + `kVibration` received at **+25 ms**, byte-identical to the official client's. **Steam inside the VM sees the gamepad**, so do the test sites, and the summary gives `branchements=1`, so no phantoms. The chain G49 -> G50 -> G52 -> G53 -> G54 -> G55 is closed. The remaining arbiter is a real GAME, which binds to ONE device (§3.39).
- **2026-08-28 (G55b)** — **G55 CONFIRMED BY TRIAL: 5 OUT OF 5, AND THE SERVER ANSWERS US.** `[C95+]` Desktop A/B of 5 alternating pairs with a uinput gamepad, a single variable. **BEFORE** (25 B cleartext register + inputs ahead of the announcement): `CHANNEL_DOWN CONTROLLER` **1,1,1,1,1** and **0 bytes received**. **AFTER** (the announcement is the channel's first byte): **0,0,0,0,0** and **5 arrivals in every session**. No exception across ten sessions. What we receive is **byte-identical to what the official client receives** — 3 `kReply` `04 00 08 03 … 01` then 2 `kVibration` `04 00 07 …`, at **+22 ms** from our announcement against +20.6 ms for it. §3.40 moves to RESOLVED, and the hypothesis "the `connect()`-ed socket filters a reply coming from another source port" is REFUTED. **Two trial traps worth remembering**: (1) the first A/B was uninterpretable because `:base+20` goes into an 8 s timeout ×4 INTERMITTENTLY, pushing the end of bootstrap from 12 s to 43 s — and the MOMENT was the variable under test; redone with `SHADOW_VIDEO_TCP=0` in both arms. (2) the `[G55] PREMIER octet` witness appears in no log: I had rebuilt `shadow-client` but **not** `shadow-test-cli`, still at G54. That does not invalidate the A/B — it tests behaviour, driven by environment variables — but it is the third time in one day that a witness compiled-but-absent cost a reading (cf. K17b on `brls::Logger::info`).
- **2026-08-28 (G55)** — **THE GAMEPAD: THE SERVER TEARS THE CHANNEL DOWN BEFORE OUR ANNOUNCEMENT LEAVES.** `[C80]` Report: `captures/tests_autonomes_20260828/G55_manette_channel_down.md` (21 agents; **all 5 real discrepancies were refuted at least once**, the retained cause by elimination and by an A/B internal to the repo). In **5 sessions out of 5** (3 desktop + 2 console), the server emits `[S56] CHANNEL_DOWN canal=CONTROLLER` **66 to 233 ms** after we put a byte on `:base+13`, and **before** our plug announcement. The official client receives NONE, in any of its three gamepad captures. **We speak between grant+12 ms and grant+299 ms; the official client does not even OPEN the socket before grant+518 ms**, sends its kPlug at grant+529 ms and receives its kReply 20.6 ms later. **Internal A/B, a single variable**: the 23 desktop sessions WITHOUT a gamepad put the same 42 B encrypted packet on the same channel with the same key, but at 7.96-19.59 s -> **0 CHANNEL_DOWN out of 23**; at ~200 ms, 57 out of 61 (S58). **The determinant is the MOMENT, not the content.** Counter-intuitive consequence: **G50's 500 ms are RIGHT** — our announcement lands exactly where the official client sends its own, the error is what PRECEDES it; do not lower `SHADOW_GAMEPAD_DELAY_MS`. This is the same family as **S57/§3.38** on audio ("our pings left ~200 ms after the channel was registered, the official client stays quiet for 7.5 s"): the mechanism had been documented for two days and had not been applied to the gamepad. Fixes: (1) `udp_register`'s **25-byte CLEARTEXT** datagram disappears from `:base+13` — the compiled default was already `false` but the SD's `settings.txt` carried `udp_register_input=1`, re-read at every start-up, so the key is **renamed** `udp_register_input_v2` to make the old value inert without touching the file; (2) G54's order guard is gated on `g_plug_envoyee_ms < 0` instead of `g_plug_arme`, which let the "gamepad not detected" case and the two external callers of `ctrl_gamepad_plug()` through. **REFUTED along the way**: the "gamepads" capability (the official client's capability field is **EMPTY**, `12 04 1a 02 0a 00`; the 7 strings live only in the telemetry JSON; the only gamepad declaration on the wire is bit `0x040` of the Authentication's permission mask `f3 = 510`, which we already send) and the encryption key (`ctrl_tcp.c` encrypts the CONTROL channel with the same `key_tx`, and that channel works end to end). **C80 and not C95+**: the server-side mechanism is not read, its binary not being in the repo. **To be proven by trial**: `[G55] PREMIER octet du canal manette` must name the announcement 5 times out of 5, and zero `CHANNEL_DOWN`.
- **2026-08-28 (K15)** — **UDP/TCP VIDEO TRANSPORT: THE SETTING, THE FIELD, AND A SECOND K13 ON OUR SIDE.** `[C95+]` Full report: `captures/tests_autonomes_20260828/K15_transport_video.md` (31-agent investigation, **15 claims refuted** along the way). **The setting has three names**: `--streaming-profile speed|reliability` (native binary), `protocol: udp|tcp` (Electron launcher), `streaming-profile` (quick menu). **The launcher only passes the argument ONE WAY** (`main.js:169`: `--streaming-profile reliability` only if TCP; nothing on UDP) — which is why it appears in NO capture. **The wire field is identified from the code, not by correlation**: `RegisterSession_<Channel>.f1(SessionInfo).f4(StreamingProtocol).f3 = NetworkType`, i.e. the `18 01` inside the `22 LL` block; UDP=0 (absent), TCP=1, QUIC=2, ordinals read from protobuf-lite's `EnumEntry` table (value written next to the name, the K14 trap avoided). Full schema of the sub-message: `f1` protoType, `f2` unidentified (video=2, cursor=4), `f3` networkType, `f4` **"channel encrypted" boolean**, `f5` port granted by the server. Verified against our own bytes: `CI_BODY_5` (video) has no f3, `CI_BODY_6` (cursor) carries `18 01` — an 8/8 partition with the channel map. **`reliability` switches video + audio-out + input** (cursor and transfer already TCP, mic stays UDP, **the gamepad has NO TCP class at all**: `GamepadChannel` derives directly from `UdpIOChannel`). **The port does not change** and no UDP video socket is created. **The framing goes from SUFP to STFP** — eight source files, no crossover, there is no `sufp_tcp_io_channel.cpp`. **The `rG` NACK disappears** (it lives in the ctor of `RemoteBitrateEstimatorIOChannel`, base class of `VideoUdpChannel` alone) **but `gE` survives**, reimplemented in the TCP classes. **AND ADAPTIVE BITRATE IS CUT OFF**: `Client::EnableDynamicBitrate` returns error -1929 if the transport is not UDP — a cost written down nowhere until now. **LIVE DEFECT ON OUR SIDE, a second occurrence of the K13 shape IN THE SAME SUB-MESSAGE**: `ctrl_msgs.c` was writing `p->profile` into **f4**, the encryption boolean, which is `20 01` on all eight captured channels. So choosing "Maximum quality" emitted `20 02` into a boolean flag instead of requesting anything at all. Fixed: f4 goes back to the byte-exact constant, and `SHADOW_VIDEO_NET_TCP=1` emits the real f3 request as opt-in (it costs a session with no picture as long as the STFP path does not exist). **Switch relevance, measured and unfavourable**: UDP loss is 0.008 to 0.099 % across two console sessions, i.e. **one truncated frame per minute**; `noyau=0` (no overflow); TCP would forbid the latency policy that already discards the oldest frame when the queue is full (64 frames dropped in 65 s); HOS's TCP buffer ceiling has never been checked (§3.30 only surveys the `udp_*` fields). The only serious argument remains the §3.34 outage — **n=1, cause unknown, and if the server cuts the subscription it would also cut a TCP stream**. Recommendation retained: instrument the outage (lower the D2 threshold from 2000 to ~300 ms, log the Wi-Fi strength that `nifmGetInternetConnectionStatus` already gives us and that we throw away) BEFORE writing a line of TCP.
- **2026-08-28 (G54)** — **THE GAMEPAD: WE WERE ANNOUNCING IN ELEVENTH PLACE.** `[C90]` Live Switch log, VM restarted just before: our client emits perfectly — detection OK, `annonce de branchement envoyee a 503 ms` (the official client's value), `branchements=1` so no phantoms. **The defect is the ORDER**: ten input messages leave BEFORE the announcement (the HID forwarder's first pass broadcasts the neutral state — all buttons released, d-pad centred — to prime the VM), so they describe a device the VM does not know about yet. But KB §3.36 says that on the official client `b1 = 0` everywhere "except on the very first message of the session", which IS the announcement. **This is not a regression from today**: G50 (yesterday) deferred the announcement by 0.5 s — the right value, measured — without deferring what came before it; the order inverted at that moment, which dates the appearance of the symptom exactly. Guard placed in `send_payload()`, the channel's only point of passage. Three hypotheses ruled out along the way, each by measurement and not by re-reading: phantom gamepads from my desktop tests (VM restarted, symptom unchanged), the loop being blocked by the G53 read (`udp_drain` is bounded and the socket non-blocking), and a detection failure (`ctrl_gamepad_present()` returns `true` unconditionally on Switch). See §3.42.
- **2026-08-28 (MEASUREMENT)** — **AUTONOMOUS CAMPAIGN ON THE DESKTOP CLIENT: 25 SESSIONS, THREE VERDICTS.** `[C95+]` Full report: `captures/tests_autonomes_20260828/RESULTATS.md`. **G52 CONFIRMED** — "annonce de branchement envoyee a 501 ms" appears on desktop for the first time, with `branchements=1` (so no phantom gamepads, G49 holds) and a correctly silent control without a gamepad; the test required a **uinput** gamepad (`tools/test/virtual_gamepad.c`), the machine having none. **G53 NOT REPRODUCED** — 2479 to 2485 messages emitted, **zero bytes received** on `:base+13` in 60 s, three times, with AND without the UDP register packet, and the read placed BEFORE any application filter; §3.40 demoted to `[C60]`, remaining hypothesis = our `connect()`-ed socket filters a reply coming from another source port, to be settled by a network capture. **V9 QUANTIFIED** — 318 to 335 short packets (len 11 and 39) per 90 s session, i.e. ~3.6/s, each one poisoning the congestion-feedback clock: a permanent defect, not a theoretical one. **V10 MEASURED** — A/B of 6 alternating pairs under induced loss: loss **0.21 % -> 0.13 %** with **6 pairs out of 6** favourable (sign test p = 0.016) and a spread that collapses (0.122-0.277 becomes 0.120-0.156); truncated frames 16.8 -> 8.8 but only 4 pairs out of 6 (inconclusive on its own); bitrate **+20 %**, so **no trace of N29's drop**. **The mechanism of the gain remains unestablished**: `chunks_redundant` is zero everywhere, but that counter only sees USELESS retransmissions (a slot already filled) — a retransmission that repairs fills a hole and never appears there. **Defect found BY the measurement**: `SHADOW_NACK_TICK=1` did not restore the old path, it cut the flush (block moved, not duplicated), so the first campaign was comparing V10 against "no retransmission at all" — fixed, campaigns rerun. **Anomaly noted, not handled**: `chunks_orphan` is ~4 % of all chunks, with `orphan_dup = orphan_lost = 0`, so all of them from the "different max_chunks" path — never looked at.
- **2026-08-28 (V10)** — **RETRANSMISSION WAS ASKING FOR CHUNKS OF A FRAME ALREADY THROWN AWAY.** `[C90]` G15 had established half the reasoning: the `rG` packet carries **no frame id at all**, so its indices can only designate the current frame ON THE SERVER SIDE, and waiting makes the request useless. But the request was still **built at flush time** — that is, after the frame had been concatenated, emitted and its buffer returned — then **sent on the congestion feedback's 50 ms beat**. At 45 fps, 50 ms is two whole frames: we were asking for chunks of a frame we had already discarded, from a server that was already encoding two more. The server did answer, though: its chunks landed in `chunks_orphan`. Three fixes. (1) The request is now built **as soon as the hole is certain** — when the LAST chunk arrives while some are still missing: at that instant the server is still on that frame (`SHADOW_NACK_AT_GAP=0` reverts to flush-only; the flush remains the safety net for a frame whose last chunk never arrives). (2) The **flush leaves the 50 ms beat**: it runs on every iteration of the receive loop (~200/s), so ~5 ms after detection; the real brake stays the 20 sends/s ceiling, not the beat (`SHADOW_NACK_TICK=1` restores it). (3) **The 32-index ceiling truncated real losses**: on one real burst, 62 chunks were missing — we asked for 32 and gave up on the other 30 **without saying anything**, so the frame stayed holed even with a perfectly cooperative server. Raised to 128 (`NACK_MAX_INDICES`), enough to cover a 512-chunk frame losing a quarter of itself; beyond that the frame is to be discarded and the IDR (G26) is the right answer. **TO BE MEASURED LIVE**: N29 has already seen a change to `rG` drop UDP throughput from 392 to 66 packets/s — the three toggles exist for that A/B.
- **2026-08-28 (V9, CORRECTED by V11)** — **THE FEEDBACK CLOCK LOCKED ONTO ANY SHORT PACKET.** `[C95+]` Bytes 6-9 of a video chunk are a send timestamp in microseconds; the offset computation was guarded by `n >= 10` **alone**, hence BEFORE header validation (`vid_wire_parse_hdr` requires n > 39). Any short beacon arriving on `:base+10` had its bytes 6-9 read as a timestamp. This is not cosmetic: `g_delay_offset_us` goes straight into the `gE` congestion-feedback message, on which the **server** sets its bitrate — an aberrant value makes it take an aberrant decision, bounded to ±1 s, which is still enormous. Now computed on a valid header and on nothing else. Still known and not fixed: the field wraps around every ~71.6 min, which produces ONE wrong measurement that the caller's bound absorbs.
- **2026-08-28 (G52)** — **THE GAMEPAD ANNOUNCEMENT COULD NOT BE SENT ON DESKTOP — two compile guards.** `[C95+]` G50 had replaced the direct send with an ARMING flushed by `ctrl_gamepad_replug_tick()`; its ONLY caller, `padforward::poll()`, has its body entirely under `#ifdef __SWITCH__`, and its single call site is itself inside a Switch block. Dead path TWICE. The VM created no gamepad, the evdev reader emitted into the void, the diagnostic showed "announcement ARMED" indefinitely — **without a single error**. Consequence: **every desktop gamepad A/B made since G50 is INVALID**, which was §3.35's missing confounder. The flush moves into `ctrl_session.c`'s receive loop (both platforms), guarded with `#ifndef __SWITCH__` so as not to double `pad_forward`'s flush and fight over G51's pulse queue, which has no lock. See §3.41.
- **2026-08-28 (G53)** — **THE SERVER WAS ANSWERING ON `:base+13` AND WE WERE NOT LISTENING.** `[C85]` The gamepad socket only ever did `send()`. The downstream direction carries three messages: `kReply` to our `kPlug` (`04 00 08 03 … [11]=01`), `kReply` to a `kUnplug` (`… 08 04 … [11]=00`), and `kVibration` (`04 00 07 …`). **The device id is 255 = "not assigned" until that `kReply` is read** — the official telemetry shows `gamepad 1 - 255 - UNDEFINED -> PLUGGING` then `gamepad 1 - 0 - PLUGGING -> PLUGGED`. Without reading it, our gamepad stays in the equivalent of PLUGGING: no announcement confirmation, no rumble routing (the official binary carries `Received vibration message for unknown remote device ID`). Three differences from video and audio, all of them traps: a **BARE** envelope `[ct 14][nonce 12][tag 16]` **with no SUFP header**; every reply sent **three times** ~10.5 ms apart, so an idempotent read is mandatory; decryption **without replay protection**, the cipher being shared with the video. Raw logging for now — an unexpected reply must be seen as it is. See §3.40.


- **2026-08-28 (K17)** — **4:4:4 WORKS ON DESKTOP — five SILENT rejections in a chain.** `[C95+]` `SHADOW_REG_F5=1` requests field 5 of the video announcement (`high_color_fidelity`, a boolean; there is NO "chroma" field on the wire at all). Full chain verified: `passerelle on_frame : format=5 1280x720 ls=[1280,1280,1280]` -> `pushYuvFrame recoit format=5` -> `4:4:4 pousse us=2560` -> `4:4:4 rendu`. **CUDA hardware decoding FAILS in 4:4:4** (`Failed setup for format cuda`), exactly as in the official client whose telemetry goes from `"mode":"CUDA"` to `"mode":"Software"` — that is inherent, not a configuration defect. Four defects fixed: (1) `pushYuvFrame` only accepted YUV420P and NV12 and discarded the rest **without a word**; (2) the renderer computed `uv_w = frame_w / 2` unconditionally, which would have stretched a quarter of the chroma image over the whole surface; (3) the U/V interleaving subsampled; (4) **the most expensive one** — `is_gpu_format` was defined as "the format is neither YUV420P nor NV12", so `yuv444p` was taken for a GPU surface; acceleration having failed, `hw_fallback_done` was true and every frame fell into a `continue` whose own comment says "silent drop". **2258 frames decoded without a single error, not one delivered.** The SWITCH path had ALREADY learnt this lesson (S16: "`hw_frames_ctx` alone — the format constant depends on the header version and must no longer serve as a test"); the desktop path had never applied it. The two are now aligned: a frame is on the GPU **if and only if** it carries a `hw_frames_ctx`.
- **2026-08-28 (K17b)** — **METHODOLOGICAL LESSON: in this protocol, the absence of an error proves nothing.** Five silent rejections followed one another in a single evening — H.264 NAL parsing applied to HEVC (always returns false), a header guard requiring `version == 0` (drops 100 % of frames), a decoder gate classifying by `b0 & 0x1F` (filters out VPS/SPS/PPS), a display entry point accepting only two formats, and an "unknown format = GPU frame" test. **NONE of them produced an error**; each simply did nothing. At every step, instrumentation (hex dump of the header, NAL-type log, per-stage entry witness) settled in ONE run what reasoning had failed to find — and two diagnoses arrived at by deduction had turned out to be WRONG before that. **Tooling corollary**: `brls::Logger::info` is filtered by level and does not appear in `borealis.log`; a diagnostic witness must go through `webrtc_log`, which has no filter. Two round trips were lost to witnesses that were compiled but mute.
- **2026-08-28 (K16)** — **H.265 WORKS — four places assumed H.264 WITHOUT SAYING SO.** `[C95+]` Picture obtained on the desktop client with `SHADOW_CODEC=1`, 907 frames assembled out of 907, `tronq=0`, 0.1 % loss. The four points, in the order they blocked: (1) **NAL parsing** (`vid_wire.c`) — a ONE-byte header and types 1/5 in H.264, against TWO bytes, a six-bit type starting at bit 1, and ALL types 0-31 in HEVC; (2) **the decoder** frozen on `AV_CODEC_ID_H264`; (3) **the VideoFrame header guard** requiring `version == 0`; (4) **the decoder's entry gate** (`h264_decoder.c::feed_nal_to_decoder`), which classified with `b0 & 0x1F` — the VPS (`0x40`) became type 0, the SPS (`0x42`) type 2, the PPS (`0x44`) type 4, all rejected, while the IDR (`0x26`) got through by the coincidence that 6 is "SEI" in H.264.
